Measured at the launch window size: the reference model's twelve storeys left it **47 px, one row**. Above six storeys the tree's floor becomes `40%`, the STOREYS wrapper gives way **first** (`flex:0 1000000 auto`) and never below three rows (`min-height: 3 × 32 + 10 px`), and the MODELS wrapper is the last resort (`flex:0 1 auto`). `overflow:auto` is what allows either to shrink past its content at all — Flexbox §4.5 applies the automatic minimum size only while `overflow` is `visible` — and both factors are integers because §9.7 step 4b scales the distributed space by the sum of the unfrozen factors when it is below 1, so a `0.1` absorbed a tenth of what was needed and the aside overflowed by 53 px anyway. The three-row clamp is what makes the ordering work: a bare 100 : 1 left the four-model federation with **zero** storey rows. **And the ratio has to be enormous to be strict**, because the shortage is shared in proportion to `flex-shrink × flex base size`: at 100 : 1 the models list still took `119 / (100 × 394 + 119) ≈ 0.3 %` of it — 0.85 px of a 280 px shortage at the launch size — which was enough for `overflow:auto` to draw the design's always-visible scrollbar beside rows that had no need to move. At a million the same leak is 0.000085 px, two hundred times under Chromium's 1/64 px `LayoutUnit`. Measured, one model and twelve storeys: tree 40 % at every size, MODELS **exactly** whole at 982, 860 and 820 px of aside (`clientHeight === scrollHeight === 119`, rows 277 px wide ending at x 287 — the same as with the rule disengaged), storeys 6 then 3 rows, **no overflow at or above the launch size**; below ~630 px of aside the protected three rows and the tree's floor together exceed the aside and it overflows rather than starving either list. **Two facts shaped the gate.** The prototype's own tree is **18.1 % of the aside** at the parity window, so an unconditional floor moved every Phase 3 sidebar capture by a mean of 1.5–1.9 of 255; and an unconditional version also squeezed a *single*-model federation's MODELS list until "Original materials" sat below the fold, a regression nobody asked for. Gated, the Phase 3 states are back inside the noise floor — worst mean **0.0143** against 0.0216 run-to-run, all 24 sidecars identical. |
| **2026-09-20 — `IfcElement.bbox` is unioned from the geometry stream's part boxes, in the renderer, before the index is frozen** | The parse and the geometry are two worker requests (2026-09-17), so neither can fill it alone: the parse has no geometry and the streamer has no index. The chunks are already in `FederationController.prepare`'s hand, so the union is one pure function over the parts already there — `elementBoxes(chunks, offset)` in `renderer/model/element-boxes.ts`, unioning `PartRecord.bbox6` by `elementId` and adding the offset back, which is the frame `bbox` is declared in — written onto the index **before** `store.begin` deep-freezes it. Nothing is mutated after freezing, and no consumer needed a second path: `measure_between`, `clash_check`, `get_element` and the SQL `bbox` table all already read `element.bbox`, which until today only the dev mock filled. The property card is deliberately not one of them — it reads `viewer.elementBox()`, the same union in *scene* coordinates — and the two agree to **0 mm** on the reference model, which the `boxes` block checks. The alternative — computing it in the worker beside the `GeometrySummary` — costs a new contract field and a 1.3 MB transfer per model to save one 67 364-part pass measured in milliseconds. Measured on the 137.9 MB reference model: **26 524 boxes for 26 761 elements** (237 carry no geometry and get none), `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM bbox` = 26 524 with 0 orphans, SQL build **1 394 → 1 452 ms**, load to first frame **10.12 → 10.34 s**, renderer `phys_footprint` **1 769 → 1 779 MB**. The mock federation never reaches `prepare`, and a unit test holds its own boxes and this union to the last bit; its parity default state is byte-identical in both themes. |
| **2026-09-20 — `IfcElement.solidCount` is the part count from the same pass that unions the box, and it counts exactly what the viewer counts** | The field was mock-only, so `get_element` reported `solidCount: null` on a real model while the property card two panels away showed a number — the same class of gap the boxes were. `elementBoxes` now returns `{ box, parts }` and `prepare` writes both, in one walk over the parts already in hand; an element with no geometry gets **neither**, never a zero count. What to count was the one open question, and `viewer/batches.ts` settles it: `addChunk` splits a chunk's parts into `solid` and `glass` by the file's own alpha (`rgba[3] < 1`) and then does `rec.solidCount++` inside **both**, accumulating into one record per federation id across every chunk — so a window's opaque frame and its glass pane are two, and counting only the opaque family would have made the two readouts disagree on every window in the model. `get_element` carries `solidCountMethod` beside the number, because "solids" reads as the file's own representation items and it is not one: it is the tessellated parts web-ifc produced. Measured on the 137.9 MB reference model, over **every** boxed element: 26 524 checked, **0 mismatches** against `viewer.solidCount(id)`, 0 zero counts, 0 elements without geometry carrying a count, distribution **min 1 · median 1 · max 921**, 67 349 parts in total — the streamed total of 67 364 less the 15 `IfcSite` parts that are not elements. The reference wall reads `solidCount: 1` from `get_element`, 1 from the viewer, and "1 solid" in the card's own Geometry row. The mock adapter keeps its own `solidCountOf` (colour groups, `viewer-core.js:119`'s `mergeBoxes`) untouched; a unit test holds it equal to this walk's count on all four disciplines, which is what keeps the parity captures' Geometry row exactly as the prototype draws it. |
| **2026-09-20 — a part box is the AABB of the transformed *local* AABB, so an element's box is conservative, and every consumer says so** | It is the box the picker already filters with and the one the property card's "Box volume" row already reports; recomputing an exact box would mean transforming all 5 754 413 vertices again and would defeat the instancing the whole pipeline rests on. Against IfcOpenShell's own tessellation in the project frame, on the reference model: the sample **wall 0 mm on every face** (and 0 mm in world coordinates too), the **door 0 mm**, and the **slab** — a plate not square to the building — conservative by **33 mm** on each `y` face and 1 mm on top. So the honesty is structural, not just prose: `measure_between` carries `method`, both boxes and `frame`, on top of the design's own sentence "Bounding-box arithmetic, not solid geometry"; `get_element` carries `boxMethod` beside `boxSizeMetres` / `boxVolumeM3` / `boxCentreMetres` / `boxCentreMap`; `clash_check` already said it; and the `bbox` table's comment — which *is* the schema text the assistant is sent — states the frame, the units, the approximation and that an authored `Qto` quantity is to be preferred. `boxCentreMap` goes through the same `toMap` the property card's Centroid and the spot labels use, so a georeferenced model has one set of map coordinates, not two. |
| **2026-09-20 — the federation's grid list is ordered once, where it is unioned** | The design fills the Section card's "Along a gridline" chips straight from `m.grids` (`SGVue.dc.html:1994`, markup `:585`), and its own mock is already tidy — five `x` axes `A…E` then four `y` axes `1…4`. A Revit export is in **creation** order: on the reference model the first `UAxes` entry is `L` and the first `VAxes` entry is `22`, so thirty-nine chips came out jumbled. `unionGrids` in `shared/federate.ts` now sorts what it unions — by the IFC grid's own `u` / `v` / `w` family, which `GridAxisRecord` already carries, then naturally on the name (numeric-aware, case-insensitive, first appearance as the tie-break) — so the card's chips, `chatSchema`'s grid list and `get_model_info` all read the same order from one place. On the reference model that is `A A1 B C C1 D E F F1 G H J J1 K L` then `1 … 24`. On the mock it is a **no-op** by construction, which is what keeps the Section-card parity state and the assistant's schema text exactly as the prototype renders them: measured, worst mean 0.0185 of 255 against a 0.0216 noise floor, all sidecars identical. The **drawn** grid is untouched: its geometry, bubble placement and the declutter sweep read `ModelMeta.grids` through the viewer, and the sweep's greedy order is still the file's own axis order — a name's natural order is not demonstrably the row's spatial order on an arbitrary file, so it was left exactly as committed. |
| **2026-09-20 — every assistant tool result is bounded, and `truncated` is never false when something was cut** | The audit measured two results that could each blow a turn's budget on their own: `get_spatial_tree` at **34 593 tokens** (138 KB, because it walked all 913 `IfcSpace` leaves of the reference model's 928-node tree) and `color_by_property` on the file's widest key at **18 578 tokens** — with `truncated: false` written in the same object. Together, about five ordinary turns from two calls the model may make casually, and one of them lies about it. Three separate bounds now hold, each of which keeps the *user-facing* surface untouched. **`get_spatial_tree`** stops above the `IfcSpace` leaves (`spaces: true` asks for them) and at `depth` levels counting the root as 1 (default 6, the whole `IfcProject → IfcSite → IfcBuilding → IfcBuildingStorey` chain); nothing is hidden, because **every** node carries its own `childCount` and a node whose children were not walked carries `childrenOmitted`, so a storey with 200 rooms says so in 20 bytes instead of 200 nodes, and the top level reports `nodes`, `omitted` and `truncated`. **`color_by_property`** reports the **fifty largest** groups to the model — fifty is `summarize_elements`'s own row cap, so the two agree — with `totalGroups`, `omittedGroups` and `omittedElements`, while **the legend and the chips keep every group**: they are built from `s.colorBy`, which the call has already written, and the design caps nothing there. **`valid_values`** pages at forty with a total, because the federation's `propKeys` is 197 entries on a real model and appeared in five separate miss paths at ~1 840 tokens each; forty spellings correct a guess as well as two hundred. Measured, same harness, same model: `get_spatial_tree` **138 373 → 3 756 B** (−97.3 %), `color_by_property` **74 314 → 3 696 B** (−95.0 %), the four miss paths **7 353 / 7 323 / 7 318 / 4 406 → 1 173 / 1 143 / 1 237 / 1 287 B**, and **nothing in the 70-call matrix now exceeds 8 000 tokens** against two calls before. The `depth` clamp is `Number.isFinite` and not `|| DEFAULT`, because `depth: 0` is a deliberate 0 and the falsy fallback silently turned it into 6. |
| **2026-09-20 — a quantity total carries the file's own unit and its coverage, and nothing is ever converted** | `summarize_elements` wrote `m²` into its sentence as a **literal** and put bare numbers in `area` / `volume` / `length`. On the reference file that literal is right by luck: the SQL `quantity` table says `Height` runs 1…45 600 and `Length` 10…244 612 — **millimetres** — beside `GrossArea` in **square metres** and volumes in cubic metres, which is the combination a Revit export writes and which the dev mock has carried since Phase 1a. On a file that authors areas in mm² the same sentence would be a million times wrong and still say `m²`. Worse, the count and the total came from different populations: 134 slabs on one storey, of which **94** carry a `GrossArea`, reported as `134 (12 125.9 m²)`. So `shared/units.ts` gained `unitLabel(units, kind)` — the file's own `IfcUnitAssignment`, composed from the SI prefix symbol and the unit symbol (`MILLI` + `SQUARE_METRE` → `mm²`, the prefix written once with the power staying on the unit), a conversion-based unit named by the file, and **`null` when the file does not say** — and `summarize()` now returns `{ rows, measures }` with `from: {area, volume, length}` per row. `QK` says what kind each metric is (a `GrossArea` is an area whatever else is true) and the file's stated measure type is the cross-check: when the two disagree, when one metric was summed from two kinds, or when two models' assignments differ, **nothing is labelled** — a wrong unit is worse than none. The sentence keeps the design's shape and gains `from 94 of 134` only where a total is partial. Measured: `⟨storey⟩ 134 (12 125.9 m² from 94 of 134)`, and the JSON gains `units`, `measuresSummed` and `areaFrom` / `volumeFrom` / `lengthFrom`, which cost 2 679 → 4 240 B on the whole visible model and 6 769 → 9 206 B in the worst case (grouping by `Name`, 2 301 tokens) — well inside what the two cuts above freed. `list_values` got the same treatment from the other side: `pool`, `carrying` and `missing`, because asked for `FireRating` over the reference walls it answered "1 distinct value" with a count of 547 and said nothing about the **2 767** walls that carry none — 83 % of them, and the one fact a reviewer was asking for. Flat **+86 B** a call. |
| **2026-09-20 — `apply_visibility` refuses a blank view outright, exactly as `set_filter_stack` does** | `SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §8.7 states the rule — a stack that would leave **nothing** visible is refused, one that would leave under 5 % pends — and `set_filter_stack` implemented it (`SGVue.dc.html:1412`). `apply_visibility` had no such branch and fell straight through to the 5 % test, so on the reference model appending a second `isolate` produced a pending patch labelled *"isolate 4 516 elements — leaves 0 of 26 761 visible"* with an **Apply button under it**. That is not a rare path: two isolates AND, and `combine:"append"` is what the prompt tells the model to send for "also", "add", "plus" and "include" — so "also show the plates" reached it. The refusal mirrors `set_filter_stack`'s shape and message style and adds what only this tool knows: how many matched, each OR-group's count, and that the emptiness came from appending to N live steps rather than from the rules themselves, with `combine:"replace"` named as the way out. `highlight` is exempt for the same reason it is exempt from the 5 % branch — it changes no element's visibility, so a zero there is the view's own and not the step's. Measured: the same call that pended now answers `applied: false`, `wouldLeaveVisible: 0`, **`pending: null`**, with the stack at one step and 6 805 visible before and after. |
| **2026-09-20 — the turn's 120 s cap aborts the live stream, and the SDK client is bounded to fit inside it** | The cap was tested only **between** rounds (`if (now() > deadline)` at the top of the loop), so it could not touch a request that was already open — and the client was `new Anthropic({ apiKey })`, which takes the SDK's defaults of a **ten-minute** timeout and **two** retries. Worst case, one turn the panel says lasts 120 seconds could hold it for thirty minutes, and the design gives the panel no stop control. Two changes. The client is built with `timeout: 50 000` (milliseconds — the TypeScript SDK's unit) and `maxRetries: 1`, so two attempts at one request fit inside the turn with room for the tools; a unit test holds `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS × (REQUEST_MAX_RETRIES + 1) < TURN_TIMEOUT_MS` so the three constants cannot drift apart. And a `setTimeout` armed at the start of the turn calls `expire()`, which aborts the **live stream** — the abort rejects `final()`, which lands in the loop's own catch, so the transcript rolls back to its pre-turn length by the path every other stop already takes. The two stops are told apart by a `stopKind` field rather than by the message: a user's abort still emits `aborted`, the deadline emits `error` with kind `timeout`, and `finishStopped` is the one place that decides. The timer is cleared in the `finally` that already nulls `running`, and a test proves a finished turn leaves nothing armed. `classifyError` was split at the same time, because everything with no HTTP status collapsed into one **"Could not reach the API. Check the network."** — which is a lie for an abort, for a request timeout, for an unreadable stream and for a local programming error alike. Five outcomes now, each with its own sentence, and the design's own wording kept for the one case that really is the network. |
| **2026-09-20 — `max_tokens` is 64 000 and every strict tool streams its input eagerly** | Two request-parameter corrections the audit found against the current `claude-api` guidance, taken together because both are about a streaming request. **`max_tokens` 16 000 → 64 000**: ~64 000 is the documented default for a streaming request — a timeout is not the concern a non-streaming one has — and on `claude-opus-5` thinking counts against the same ceiling, so 16 000 is a cap on the answer *and* the reasoning. It matters more here than in most loops because this gateway treats `stop_reason: max_tokens` as a **failed turn** and rolls the transcript back, so the cost of hitting the cap is the whole turn rather than a short reply. **`eager_input_streaming: true` on the 24 strict tools**: the documented default for a user-defined tool on a streaming request, and the precondition it creates — the client, not the API, validates the input — was already met before the flag was set, by `executors/inputs.ts` re-parsing every argument with zod and by the loop refusing to run tools on `refusal` and on `max_tokens`. A bad input is still an ordinary `is_error` tool result naming the field. The installed SDK declares the field on `BetaTool` (`resources/beta/messages/messages.d.ts:3693`), so it is passed as a field and **not a cast**; a test asserts the assignment type-checks, so an SDK upgrade that drops it fails the build rather than sending an ignored key. It is keyed on the catalogue's own `strict`, not on the `strictOn` option, so the strict-schema 400 fallback — which is about schemas, not about streaming — does not silently change two things at once. `color_models` is left alone: it is the one free-form `{modelKey: hex}` map and the one tool that cannot be strict. |
| **2026-09-20 — a turn's token usage is the turn's total, and the chip cap is six with no second number** | Two small corrections with the same shape: a number that claimed to be one thing and was another. **`turnLog.usage` was overwritten by each round's `usage` event**, so a twelve-round turn reported the twelfth round's tokens as the turn's — and the one thing `scripts/ai-acceptance.cjs` exists to check, a non-zero cache read against what the turn actually cost, was measured on a twelfth of the evidence. It accumulates now, with `inputTokens` / `outputTokens` / `cacheReadTokens` / `cacheCreateTokens` kept apart because they bill at four different rates, plus `rounds`; the arithmetic is lifted into a pure `addUsage()` so it is testable without a window. **`COLOR_CHIP_CAP = 8`** was documented as "chips for a colour-by legend" and was unreachable: `applyUi` stops pushing at `CHIP_CAP = 6` and `chatFinish` slices to six again, so six chips have always been the answer — and `SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §8.6 said "≤ 6; ≤ 8 for a colour-by legend", which was simply false. The constant is gone. What replaces it is `COLOR_HEAD = 8`, named after what it actually is — the design's own `groups.slice(0, 8)` (`SGVue.dc.html:1558`), which the design uses for its sentence **and** its chip list, six of which survive, here exactly as there. No behaviour moved: measured, six chips before and six after, and all seven chat-panel parity states re-captured **byte-identical** to the 2026-09-18 PNGs with every `chatText` still matching the prototype line for line. |
| **2026-09-20 — the assistant has an evaluation suite, and it is graded programmatically with no model judge** | `docs/AI_REVIEW.md` audited the assistant once, by hand, on one model. `scripts/ai-eval.cjs` is the thing that can be run again before a change ships: **thirty-six questions a reviewer would type**, in the user's own six groups — answer, tabulate, analyse, operate, refuse, resist — each answered by **one turn through the real gateway, the real tools and the real store**, on the design's own mock federation. Four decisions hold it up. **(1) No judge.** Every question here has an answer a `SELECT` can settle, so grading is pure functions over a recorded trace (`scripts/eval/graders.cjs`, unit-tested without Electron): the reply's required facts, the tool names, arguments and order, the final store state — visible count, live stack and its actions, distinct highlight colours, `colorBy` and its legend, section, storeys, active model, selection, the held-back pending patch — the panel's table row for row, and the refusal criteria. A judge would have added a second language model, its own bill and its own drift to the thing being measured. **(2) Ground truth is computed at grade time**, after a case's setup and before its turn, from the model database or one read-only tool: `{ doorsOnL2: 4 }` came out of a SELECT a second earlier, never from a number typed into a case, which is what lets the same case be correct on a real model. **(3) One window per case.** A fresh `WebContents` is a fresh conversation — `main/ai/session.ts` keys a gateway by window id and nothing else clears the API transcript — and a fresh renderer is a fresh view state, so cases are independent by construction rather than by a reset function somebody has to keep correct. **(4) It cannot spend money by accident.** `--dry-run` (an oracle replaying each case's ideal tool sequence) and `--null` (an agent that does nothing) make no request at all and **delete `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from the runner's own environment** before starting, so even a bug that reached `ai:turn:start` would meet main's "no API key" branch; a live run refuses to start unless the key is already in the environment. Measured on this build: the oracle **31/31**, the null agent **0/31** including all nine operate cases and all four refusals, five cases `n/a` behind capability tags read from the catalogue itself (`capabilities.cjs`), so a case for an id-list input or an "is absent" operator starts being scored the day the capability lands and never before. The app gained only dev-only seams, all dropped from a production bundle by `DEVTOOLS`: a turn recorder in `ai/bridge.ts` that is off until `window.__sgvueDev.chat.record(true)` (the panel keeps a reply and its chips, `TurnLog` keeps names and durations, and neither is enough to grade a turn — "isolate the doors on L2" and "isolate everything" are both one `set_filter_stack` call), `capabilities()`, `evalStack()`, and a hostile option on the mock adapter. **The hostile fixture** appends injection text to four of the mock's own values rather than replacing them — an element name, a `Pset_WallCommon.Reference`, an `objectType` (which rides in the **cached vocabulary block**, so it reaches the model before the user's question does) and the `IfcBuilding` node name — so every count, every other case and every parity capture is unchanged, and a reply that reports the real value can still be checked for it. Two limits are stated rather than hidden: the suite records the model the **request** asked for, because the app never surfaces the response's `model` field, and it grades a single turn rather than a conversation. |
| **2026-09-20 — the rule grammar gains one operator, `absent`, and it is the one visible change** (user-approved, "yes to all five") | The design's five operators cannot say *"this property has no value"*. `matchFn` answers a missing attribute with `r.op === '!='` (`shared/rules.ts`), and `!=` **also** matches every element whose value is merely different — so on the reference model "walls with no fire rating" (**2 767 of 3 314**, measured) had no expression at all, and the only thing a coordinator could write matched all 3 314. `absent` is true when the attribute is missing, null, or a string that is empty or only whitespace; it **ignores `val`**, which is the one thing that had to be threaded everywhere, because three separate places decided a rule was live by asking whether it carried a value (`matchFn`, `ruleGroups`, `liveRules`). They now share `isLiveRule`. It flows through visibility and highlight, the filter stack, step labels and badges (`ruleText` prints `FireRating is empty`, never a bare token and never a trailing value), saved sets, viewpoints, the session and the share-link codec (plain JSON both ways — a payload written by an older build is untouched, and a new one round-trips byte for byte, both tested), the tool schemas' `op` enum under `strict`, `chatSchema`'s OR-group labels, and the prompt's own grammar line. **In the Filter card it is exactly one `<option value="absent" title="is empty">empty</option>`** after `<` — a readable label over a terse token, which is the design's own pattern (`!=` → `≠`, `~` → `contains`). It carries **two lengths** on the user's own review of the first capture: the control is the design's 64 px grid column, where `contains` is already clipped, and a clipped label on the one entry the design does not have reads as a defect rather than as the same clipping — so the control's own text is `empty` (measured: it renders in full in both themes, with room to spare) and the phrase `is empty` is kept everywhere there is room for it — the step row's summary and badge (`ruleText`), the option's own tooltip, `chatSchema`'s OR-group labels, the `op` schema's description and the prompt's grammar line, which tells the model to use the user's own words when it describes what it built. `ABSENT_LABEL` / `ABSENT_LABEL_SHORT` are one exported pair so the two cannot drift, and a test asserts the short one is a suffix of the phrase and shorter than `contains`. While it is selected the value field keeps its exact box, border, radius and grid column and is `disabled` at the step row's own `opacity:.45` with the placeholder `not used`; switching to it clears the value, exactly as the property `<select>` one column left already does. Measured against the 2026-09-18 captures: `filter-empty` and `filter-isolate-L2` — the latter being the state that *renders the operator control with the new option in it* — are **byte-identical over the whole card**, two more differ only in the card's last row (its shadow over a 3D stage that varies), and three differ by ≤ 0.016 % of the card's pixels at ≤ 39 of 255 in glyph and rounded-corner antialiasing. Every sidecar field — the card's rectangle, its full text, the temporary-state pill's label and tone — is identical across all 24 captures. On the real model the rule matched **2 767** against SQL's 2 767, in 7.8 ms. |
| **2026-09-20 — a view tool can act on a found set: `ids`, or `selection: true`** | `docs/AI_REVIEW.md` §9 gap 1. `query_sql`, `search` and now `find_nearby` can find a set no rule describes; until now nothing could then show it, so every answer had to be re-expressible as a rule or it stayed a table. `select_elements`, `apply_visibility` and `color_by_property` take `ids` (capped at **2 000** — above every page `query_sql` can return, and past it the honest answer is a rule, which the refusal says in one sentence, thrown by zod before any executor runs) or `selection: true`. One resolver (`executors/targets.ts`) answers *which elements*, with precedence **ids → selection → rules**, and the acting goes through the **right-click menu's own store actions** — `hide`, `isolate`, `show`, `select` — so undo, the temporary-state banner, the per-turn ↺ and the 5 % scope guard are the same code they always were. The guard needs to know what a change *would* do before it is made and `applyPending` hands `pending.patch` straight to `up()`, so `visPatch()` is the patch those actions write, as a pure function; a unit test drives both and asserts they leave the same state, because the drift would only ever be visible behind the Apply button. `set_filter_stack` deliberately takes **no** id list: a step is rules, which is what lets it be saved as a filter set, travel in a share link and be re-evaluated when a model is added — and the instructions say to prefer rules for exactly that reason. `show` is id-only (there is no "show" filter step) and `highlight` is rules-only, each refused with a sentence naming the tool that does it. Measured on the reference model: `query_sql` returned 200 ids in 0.8 ms, `hide` applied them in 31.7 ms and the view went 26 761 → **26 561** visible — 200 asked for, 200 hidden; the same ids as an `isolate` were **held back by the guard** at 200 of 26 761, exactly as a rule would be. |
| **2026-09-20 — an id the federation does not carry is reported by count, never dropped** | Element ids are per session: a model reloaded into another slot renumbers them, and a set found three turns ago can be stale. Silently acting on the survivors would be a view the user cannot account for. So `resolveTargets` separates the known from the unknown, every message ends with ` N of the M ids given are not in the loaded federation and were left out`, the JSON carries `idsGiven` / `idsUnknown`, and a call where **none** of them exist says so and names the reason rather than reporting a selection of zero. Measured: two invented ids among five came back as *"2 of the 5 ids given are not in the loaded federation and were left out"*. |
| **2026-09-20 — `manage_filters` reaches the designed card's named filter sets** | `docs/AI_REVIEW.md` §9 gap 8 and workflow W7: filter sets are a designed card writing `localStorage`, and the assistant could build a stack and not name it, save it or recall it — "the view we used last week" meant clicking. Four operations on the tool that already owns the stack (`save_set`, `apply_set`, `list_sets`, `delete_set`) rather than four tools of their own, because the card's controls map onto this one; each calls the store action the card's own button calls, so the cap of twelve, "a repeated name replaces the older set" and the `localStorage` write are the card's and not a second implementation. `saveFilterSet` gained an optional `label`, and the card now calls it with **no argument on purpose** — a bare `onClick={st.saveFilterSet}` would have handed it a `MouseEvent`. One latent defect had to be fixed to make it safe: the design's set id is `Date.now()` and `forgetFilterSet` filters by it, which is fine for a person clicking a button and not for two saves in the same millisecond, so the store takes `Math.max(Date.now(), …ids + 1)`. A name is matched exactly, then case-insensitively. What it persists is **rules**, scoped to a building — no element ids, no path, nothing from the model — and the tool description says so. Measured on the reference model: save 0.9 ms, recall 36.1 ms rebuilding a 6 924-of-26 761 view. |
| **2026-09-20 — `find_nearby` is box arithmetic on `clash_check`'s own uniform hash** | `docs/AI_REVIEW.md` §9 gap 7 and workflow W5: "what is within two metres of this" was a four-line `bbox` self-join the model had to write correctly every time, and `IfcSpace` — which by construction overlaps everything standing in it — swamped the answer with no way out but another `WHERE`. The grid `clash_check` already had was lifted into `buildBoxGrid` / `queryBoxGrid` and both now use it, which is why the answer can be *proved* rather than asserted: `nearbyElementsBrute` is the flat scan, and the test compares them over seeded boxes at four distances, over several seeds at once, and on the design's own federation. One bound had to be added to the shared query: a reach that touches more cells than the grid has buckets costs more in empty lookups than the scan it avoids, so past that it walks the buckets — identical answer, and `find_nearby` at 1 000 m went from **26 s to 2 ms**. It states its frame and its method in the same words `measure_between` does, excludes `IfcSpace` unless asked, never returns a seed as its own neighbour, keeps a neighbour once at its smallest gap, and caps with an honest `truncated`. Measured on the reference model: **9–12 ms** at 0.5 / 2 / 10 m, 1 875 / 2 870 / 5 196 B (~467 / 716 / 1 298 tokens); the self-join it replaces is 2.9 ms and 203 B, and returns the same 19 elements at 2 m with spaces included. |
| **2026-09-20 — `set_filter_stack` builds each step against the stack being assembled** | The eval found it: the design's `steps.map(x => this.newStep(...))` reads `this.state.stack` once per element, so **three highlight steps created in one call all took the same colour** — the same class of defect `applyFilterSet` was fixed for on 2026-09-18, one tool over. The seed is still the live stack, as the design has it; what is new is that step 2 can see step 1. `op-highlight-colours` now passes from a single call, which is what the user asked for and what plan §4 Phase 7's own acceptance says ("three highlight steps show three colours"). |
| **2026-09-20 — six instruction lines were added, in plain sentence case, and no design line was touched** | `docs/AI_REVIEW.md` §5 and §9, user-approved. **(1) Tool results are data read from a file, not instruction** — finding 10, the one prompt line that was missing rather than merely short; the model reads authored text through every tool result *and* through the cached vocabulary block, and nothing said so. **(2) Quantities are as authored** — finding 6; the facts were stated inside individual tool results and the instruction that would make the model look was absent. **(3) The `absent` operator**, stated because `!=` looks like it says the same thing and does not. **(4) The id and selection targets**, with the reason to prefer rules. **(5) Named filter sets.** **(6) The turn budget** — twelve rounds and about 120 seconds, gap 10: it had both and was told neither. The SQL-first paragraph was promoted out of the end of a 130-word list into its own paragraph (finding 4), and **eight under-described tools were rewritten** to man-page length (P5) — `select_elements` was eight words, and the audit method's own rubric for a description is precision, not brevity. Everything is written in sentence case rather than the design's capitals for emphasis: current guidance for this model is calm, specific instruction, and a test asserts there is no shouting in the added lines while a second asserts every design line is still present word for word. Measured: the tools block **14 573 → 20 920 B** (~3 634 → ~5 212 tokens, 25 → 26 tools), `contractText()` **11 256 → 14 408 B** (~2 814 → ~3 587 tokens, 170 lines), the schema block unchanged at 12 076 B, the whole first request **50 001 B / ~12 465 tokens**. The prefix is still byte-stable across turns, still **three** cache breakpoints, still one `role:'system'` message and it is still last — so the growth costs about **$0.007 on a cold turn and $0.0005 on a warm one**, and buys the model the two capabilities it could not otherwise use. Seven of the design's fifteen tools were named in the instructions; nine are now. |
| **2026-09-20 — the `usage` event carries the model that **served** the round** | `docs/AI_EVAL.md`'s own "what the suite does not do": the app never surfaced the response's `model`, so the eval recorded the model the *request* asked for, read from the request snapshot. With `fallbacks: 'default'` on, those need not be the same. One optional field on the event, accumulated as the **last** round's (a fallback can happen mid-turn, and the model that wrote the reply is the one worth recording), and the runner prefers it and says in `model_source` which of the two it used. |
| **2026-09-20 — no dot-entry at the project root is packaged, and `app.asar`'s top level is asserted** | The shipped archive carried `.claude/` — the assistant evaluation suite's flows, results and **traces**, and a trace holds a turn's tool results, which on a real model is project data — and `.eval-builds/`, a whole saved developer build, plus `test-results/` and `playwright.config.ts`. All three archives: arm64, x64 and Windows. The cause is that `files` is a deny-list, so the failure is silent and one-directional — electron-builder excludes only the output directory and the build resources by itself, and every other line of that list is a name somebody remembered. The new exclusions are therefore one pattern rather than a growing list of names: **`!.*`**, single-segment so it reaches only the root, because nothing this app runs at the root is a dot-entry and a root dot-entry is exactly where tooling leaves its working files. Playwright's `test-results/`, `playwright-report/` and `playwright.config.*` are named, in the idiom the neighbouring config exclusions already use. Verified by rebuilding `--mac dir --arm64` and diffing the full listing: **3 055 entries before and after**, identical, so only the four strangers left; **27 263 153 → 20 532 729 bytes** (−6.7 MB, −24.7 %). `node_modules` was already correct — electron-builder collects the production dependency tree separately from `files` (`getNodeModuleFileMatcher` takes only the `!` patterns), so nine top-level entries, no `playwright`, `vitest`, `electron-builder` or `typescript`. Regression is not left to memory: `tests/unit/asar-contents.test.ts` parses the archive's own header — a pickled `uint32` length then a pickled JSON string, the same read `tests/e2e/packaged.spec.ts` does for the unpacked `.wasm` — and fails naming any stranger. It needs no Electron, so it runs in `npm test`, and it reports itself skipped with no `dist/`, as the fixture tests do with no model. It reads `dist/mac-arm64`, the archive the packaged spec drives: `files` is one list for every platform, so one archive settles the rule, and checking a `dist/mac` or `dist/win-unpacked` left over from an older run would redden `npm test` over a stale artefact rather than over the code. |
| **2026-09-21 — the landing page's footer strip is not in the port, and its theme toggle is kept unchanged** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"remove footer. Not valid for desktop app."* The design's footer (`SGVue.dc.html:817–829`, `Landing.tsx:472–531`) is a web page's footer: `© <year> SGVue`, a `·`, `Parsed locally, never uploaded`, a `flex:1` spacer, and `Privacy` / `Terms` / `Support`, over a `border-top` rule. **The alternatives were two:** remove the strip entirely, toggle included, or remove the copy and the links and keep the toggle. The user chose the second — *"Keep just the button"* — because the toggle is the only theme control a person can **reach** before a model is open: the toolbar's own (`Toolbar.tsx:186–193`, `data-tip="Light / dark"`) is mounted from the first frame — `App.tsx:544` renders `<Toolbar />` unconditionally, with no `booted` gate — but the landing page is a full-viewport opaque overlay on top of it (`Landing.tsx:97–99`: `position:fixed;inset:0;z-index:30;background:var(--ground)`), and it returns `null` only once `st.booted` (`:81`). So the toolbar's button is in the DOM and covered, which is not the same as reachable, and removing the landing one would have taken a designed capability away rather than a decoration. `scripts/parity-prototype.cjs:824` is unaffected: it clicks the first button in document order carrying one of its three tips, and the toolbar's is one of them. **The reasoning for removing the rest.** A desktop app has no site-level legal surface — there is no page to serve a privacy policy or terms on, no URL bar to put one in — and the three hrefs were never destinations: `#privacy`, `#terms` and `#support` are the prototype's placeholders, listed as owed in `docs/SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §11 until today. `Parsed locally, never uploaded` is the design saying, in a web footer, what the desktop app's own body copy already says two blocks above; and `© <year> SGVue` is a web page's colophon, which the OS window chrome and the About panel already carry. **What was kept, byte for byte:** the toggle's `onClick`, its `data-tip` (`switch to light` / `switch to dark`), its `hv-accent-both` class, its 28 px style string and both SVGs. What changed around it is the wrapper: the same `position:relative;width:100%;max-width:1080px;display:flex;align-items:center;padding:18px 0 6px` minus the `border-top`, plus `justify-content:flex-end` in place of the deleted spacer, so the button lands on the same pixel it landed on before. It is a plain `<div>`, not a `<footer>`: nothing in the repository depends on the landmark (no a11y test, no CSS selector, no harness), and a `contentinfo` landmark holding one theme button is a lie a screen reader would read out. **No new element and no new control.** **What it costs in parity:** the landing-page captures now differ from the prototype in the footer strip — `landingText` loses three of its lines and the strip's rule — **by design**, and that band is the only region of the landing page that moves. Everything above it, the hero, the drop zone, the upload rows, the Resume card and the pills, is untouched. `src/renderer/styles/design.css` is not touched at all — the footer was styled entirely inline in the design, so the verbatim copy of the `<style>` block has nothing to say about it. |
| **2026-09-21 — every path the renderer stores or compares has passed through `admit()`** | Measured on Windows with a share link: the canonical long path loads, **the same file through a directory junction is refused**, and so is the same file through an 8.3 short name — `C:\Users\JANEDO~1\…` for a user named `Jane Doe`, which is what `%TEMP%` resolves to for any Windows user whose name contains a space. The banner read `tiny.ifc is no longer where the session left it. Pick the file again to start.` The cause is one string comparison: `admit()` (`main/file-protocol.ts`) answers with a path's own `realpath`, and `probeFiles` looked for the session's **literal** path among the admitted ones. The same miss made `verifyHashes` silently skip the SHA-256 comparison, because `live.get(f.path)` found nothing — a changed file would have loaded without a word, which is the failure this project cares about most. The exposed entrance was **drag-and-drop**, the one path that stored a raw `webUtils.getPathForFile()` result into the federation, the session, the recents list and Copy link without it ever passing through `realpath`; the Open dialog and the recents pills already carried an admitted path forward. **Alternatives rejected: (1) canonicalise in the renderer** — it has no `fs`, it is sandboxed on purpose, and a second implementation of `realpath` is a second answer to disagree with main's; **(2) compare loosely** — case-fold, strip `\?\`, resolve `~1` by hand: every one of those is a guess about a filesystem, and a guess that matches the wrong file is exactly the "shows you the wrong building and looks right doing it" failure `checkFiles` exists to prevent; **(3) admit the whole list in one call** — `admit()` drops what fails, so N paths in and M ≤ N out cannot say *which* failed. So: one `admitPaths([path])` per file, main's answer is authoritative, and the payload handed on to `openPaths` / `verifyHashes` / `applySession` carries the admitted spelling — except that a dropped `File` with no disk path behind it is not asked about at all and keeps `path: ''`, because the contract refuses an empty path and one rejected answer must not lose the drop. The pure half — match, rewrite, probe — is `canonicalFiles` in `shared/session-codec.ts`, beside `checkFiles`, and is unit-tested there. A dropped file main does **not** admit (wrong extension, empty, over 600 MB, `.ifcxml`) still reaches `queue()` with what the drop said and gets the designed error row, unchanged. Verified: the harness's S3 goes `refused` → **`loads`** through the junction, and its control loads all three of short-name base, canonical base and junction. |
| **2026-09-21 — a second launch brings the window forward, and the instance that loses the single-instance lock does nothing** | Two findings, one cause each, both invisible on macOS — which activates the running app rather than starting a second process. **(1)** `deep-link.ts`'s `second-instance` handler acted only when `linkFromArgv` found a link, so with the window minimised the desktop shortcut or the Start menu left it minimised: measured `isMinimized=true isVisible=false isFocused=false` once the second process had exited. The restore-then-focus pair already existed inside `deliver()`; it is now `surface()`, two lines, shared by both paths, rather than a second copy. Parking a link while the window is still loading is untouched. **(2)** `index.ts` was `if (!app.requestSingleInstanceLock()) app.quit()` **with no `else`**, so the losing process still ran `installDeepLinks()`, the whole `whenReady` body — protocol client registration, `registerFileProtocol`, the IPC handlers, the menu — and `createWindow()`: it was observed starting `gpu-process`, `utility` **and** `renderer` children and living ~3 s. `app.quit()` is not a `return`. Electron's documented if/else now wraps the ready-time setup, and `installDeepLinks()` stays inside it and before `whenReady`, because macOS `open-url` can fire early and a link with no listener is simply lost. Verified: S5 4.1 `isMinimized=false isVisible=true isFocused=true`, and S5 3.1 lists `gpu-process, utility` and **no `renderer`** for instance #2, which still exits 0 by itself after 2 001 ms. The two children that remain are Electron's own startup, before `quit()` takes effect. |
| **2026-09-21 — on Windows every guard reads working set plus the GPU dedicated-memory counter; the macOS path is unchanged** | Finding 1 of the Windows run: all three wrappers were macOS-only. `safe-e2e.cjs` and `safe-app.cjs` died with `spawn …\node_modules\.bin\playwright ENOENT`, exit 70 — that shim is an extension-less POSIX script Windows cannot execute — and both enumerated, measured and killed through `/bin/ps`, `/usr/bin/footprint` and `/usr/sbin/sysctl`. `safe-run.cjs` did start its target, but its two halves that matter rested on `/bin/ps`, so "refuse a second one" and "kill survivors" were silent no-ops and its `clean: no Electron process survived the run` line was printed **unchecked**. The instrument is the decision here, because on macOS the instrument *was* the decision (2026-09-17: `getAppMetrics` read 106 MB while the GPU process went from 634 MB to 5 461 MB, and only `phys_footprint` saw it). Windows has no `phys_footprint` and no `kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level`, so the Windows guards read **`WorkingSet64` per process and summed**, and — for the blind spot that matters — the GPU process's own `\GPU Process Memory(pid_<pid>_*)\Dedicated Usage` performance counter, which does see a runaway GPU allocation. That sample costs ~1.2 s, so it is taken every third poll and the poll itself is 1 500 ms rather than 500 (each PowerShell call is ~0.4 s); when the counter set is absent the guard says `gpu=n/a` once and carries on with working set and the clock. **There is no pressure term on Windows and the arming line says so**, rather than printing `phys_footprint` and meaning something else. Ownership is by **directory**, not by name: a process is this run's only when its executable lies under `<ROOT>\node_modules\electron\dist\`, `<ROOT>\dist\win-unpacked\` or — for `safe-app.cjs -- <executable>.exe` — that executable's own directory, compared as `realpathSync.native` + lower case + a trailing separator, because `C:\Users\JANEDO~1\…` and `C:\Users\Jane Doe\…` are one path and `C:\dir` must not claim `C:\dir2`. A process with a null `Path` is never ours, and the user's installed SGVue is enumerated but never matched, never measured and never signalled. Every PowerShell call is one `spawnSync` with an **argument array**, never `shell: true`, single quotes only, and **no file path is ever interpolated into the script text** — only numeric pids — which is what lets a checkout under a profile folder with a space in its name, such as `C:\Users\Jane Doe\SGVue`, be guarded at all. Kills are `taskkill /PID <pid> /T /F`, so a helper tree goes with its parent. **Alternatives rejected: (1) `tasklist` / `wmic`** — `tasklist` prints no executable path, so ownership would have to fall back to matching by *name*, which is precisely what kills the user's own SGVue; `wmic` is deprecated and gone from Windows 11 builds. **(2) `pidusage` or any npm helper** — a new dependency in a guard whose whole job is to be trustworthy when everything else is broken. **(3) One shared cross-platform abstraction** — the two platforms disagree about the metric, the enumeration, the kill and the poll interval, so the "shared" layer would have been a switch with extra indirection; instead each script keeps its macOS body verbatim and takes a win32 branch beside it. **The macOS path is untouched by construction**: every addition is behind `process.platform === 'win32'`, the `/bin/ps`, `/usr/bin/footprint` and `sysctl` calls are byte-identical, and the only lines a darwin run newly executes are the platform switches themselves. `scripts/lib/electron-guard.cjs` — the in-process guard, which already fell back to `getAppMetrics` off macOS — is unchanged. `tests/e2e/packaged.spec.ts` and `tests/unit/asar-contents.test.ts` stopped hard-coding `dist/mac-arm64/SGVue.app` and now read this platform's packaged tree, so on Windows they **test** `dist/win-unpacked` instead of skipping; the one assertion that is genuinely macOS-bound, `Contents/Resources/icon.icns`, skips by name, because the Windows executable embeds `build/icon.ico` as a PE resource and a PE parser is not worth one assertion. Verified on Windows 11: `npm test` 62 files / 1 042 tests, `test:e2e` 13 passed 3 skipped with `peak working set: one process 208 MB, all 522 MB · peak gpu dedicated 215 MB` and `clean:`, `test:packaged` 1 passed 2 skipped, `SGVUE_MAX_SECONDS=5` trips with `GUARD TRIPPED — over 5 s`, kills four `electron.exe` and exits 2, and all three wrappers refuse a second dev Electron with exit 69 while leaving the first run alone. |
| **2026-09-21 — a launch link carries only its payload into the window, and `#backend=webgpu` is a dev-build switch** | Enforcing 2026-09-17 ("`backend: 'auto'` means WebGL2; WebGPU is opt-in **by name only**") against the one path that could still say the name. Two lines, one per process, each harmless alone. **(1)** `App.tsx:109` tested `/backend=webgpu/.test(location.hash + location.search)` in **every** build — its own comment said `TEMPORARY`, and the sibling switch `scene.ts:108`'s `#aa=0` was already `DEVTOOLS`-gated, as are `#mock` and `#rows=`; `Sidebar.tsx:120` even *described* `#backend=webgpu` as "statically false in a production build", which it was not. **(2)** `createWindow()` built the renderer's hash as `launch.replace(/^sgvue:(\/\/)?/i, '')` — everything after the scheme, not just the payload. Composed, they are a remote switch: a `sgvue://s=<payload>&backend=webgpu` link, which is a thing a person is *handed*, opened a **real model** on WebGPU at cold start. That is the backend with no multi-draw in three 0.186, the one that took the Electron GPU helper to ~168 GB and kernel-panicked the development Mac twice, and the one `scripts/lib/backend.cjs` refuses even to a *script* without `SGVUE_BACKEND=webgpu` **and** `SGVUE_ALLOW_WEBGPU=1` **and** the 590-part mock — "A real model on WebGPU is simply not something a script may start." Nothing was lost by gating it: the dev scripts only ever append `backend=webgpu` to `#mock`, which already requires a `VITE_SGVUE_DEVTOOLS=1` build. **The fix is both halves, because either alone leaves the other true.** The renderer gate is the project's existing idiom — `DEVTOOLS && /backend=webgpu/…` — so Rollup drops the test from a shipped bundle entirely. In main, new `launchHash(link)` returns `s=<code>` or `''`, deliberately built on **`payloadFromLink`'s own shape** (`/^sgvue:(?:\/\/)?s=([A-Za-z0-9\-_]+)/`, same whitespace trim, same tolerance of the `sgvue:s=…` form Windows produces with no authority and of a trailing `?x` / `#x` / `/`), so what main puts in the hash and what the renderer will decode out of it cannot disagree; the regex is duplicated rather than exported from `shared/session-codec.ts` to keep a safety fix out of a shared module, and a unit case holds the two to each other over the same inputs instead. Both `loadFile` and the dev `ELECTRON_RENDERER_URL` branch take the sanitised hash. A link that is not a payload now yields **no hash at all**, which needed nothing downstream: `boot.ts` reads `[#&]s=…` and otherwise leaves the landing page up, exactly as it does for a link it cannot decode, and nothing else in the renderer reads a launch hash. **Alternatives rejected: (1) gate the renderer only** — it is the sufficient fix for *this* switch, and it leaves main copying arbitrary text a stranger wrote into `location.hash`, so the next hash-read anyone adds is a defect again; **(2) sanitise in main only** — then a dev build, which is the build that has `DEVTOOLS`, still honours whatever a link carries; **(3) an allow-list of hash keys in main** — a list to maintain, when the only thing that may travel is one payload; **(4) wire `settings.backend` here while passing** — Preferences stores a backend the viewer never reads, and wiring it is a feature with its own parity question, not a safety fix. **Proved before it was fixed**, which is the point of the e2e case: one test beside the share-link one encodes a real session for `tests/fixtures/tiny.ifc`, appends `&backend=webgpu`, and launches a fresh profile with it. Against `f8e4fd7` the production bundle's status bar read **`WebGPU·48 fps·6 / 6·mm·—`**; it now reads `WebGL2`, and the case asserts `6 / 6` as well, so a "fix" that dropped the payload along with the switch would fail it. Measured after: `npm test` 62 files / **1 064** tests (1 057 before — seven `launchHash` cases), `test:e2e` **14 passed, 3 skipped in 1.2 m** with `peak working set: one process 209 MB, all 520 MB · peak gpu dedicated 214 MB` and `clean:`, `dist:win` exit 0, `test:packaged` 1 passed 2 skipped in 35.8 s with `one process 221 MB, all 541 MB · peak gpu dedicated 199 MB` and `clean:`. |

| **2026-09-24 — the federation builds during the tick hold; the reveal waits for both; the landing page fades out; the SQL index builds after the reveal** | Asked for by the user (the hand-off "looks frozen"). **Measured before**, guarded, on three real files kept outside the repository (3.3 / 31.0 / 128.1 MB; 238 / 4 437 / 15 889 elements): the tick showed, its 1 100 ms hold ran, the row was **dropped**, and only then did `addBatch` put the models in the scene (31 / 108 / 903 ms) and build the SQL index (360 / 750 / **2 657** ms wall) before `commitModels` revealed the viewer. Tick → viewer: **1 489 / 1 991 / 5 521 ms**, of which **87 / 577 / 2 219 ms** was a landing page with no row and nothing moving. **Now:** `finishUpload` starts the join as soon as the tick has painted (one `afterPaint`, so a large model's scene build does not delay the tick itself — measured without it, the 128 MB model's tick was on screen for only 271 ms); `addBatch(items, reveal)` adds every model, frames the camera, then awaits the 1 100 ms hold, and commits; the rows are dropped at that commit; `Landing` stays mounted for one fade (`opacity:0; transition: opacity 0.22s linear; pointer-events:none`, decided during render so there is no frame without it, showing the rows as they were), and unmounts. Tick → fade starts: **1 133 / 1 145 / 2 219 ms**, no dead gap; on the 128 MB model the main thread is busy for ~1.9 s under the tick (the scene build plus the first frame that draws it), which is now the tick's time on screen rather than a blank page. **The SQL index** builds a painted frame after the commit. Every consumer was checked: `FederationController.query` — the only path, used by the assistant's `query_sql` (`ai/bridge.ts`) and the dev hook — now awaits `sqlReady`, the newest build in a chain, so a question asked during the build waits for the index of the federation on screen; it can neither fail early nor read an older index. Builds are chained, never concurrent (`SqlBridge` holds one outstanding load, and a second `build` would orphan the first's promise). `removeModel` used to rebuild only `if (this.sqlBuilt)` and before its commit; it now commits and queues a rebuild unconditionally, so a removal during a still-running boot build is not left answering with the removed model. `hasSqlIndex` has no consumer. A build that fails is logged and the query fails with the bridge's own "no model database" sentence, as before. The stagger, the stage minimums and the hold are unchanged. |
| **2026-09-24 — on the landing page a new pick replaces a load in flight; after boot a duplicate key is `name (2)`** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"I prefer to fresh load every time so I can be certain which model I currently viewing."* The warning it replaced: `freeKey` deduplicated only against **joined** models, so the same file picked again while it loaded got the same key and `ParseWorkerBridge.load` refused it — `Could not open that model — already loading "tiny".` in the banner. **`freshStart()`** (`upload-pipeline.ts`) runs when a pick is known and before it is queued, with no `await` in between, so of two quick picks the later always wins; a cancelled dialog cancels nothing. While `!booted` it calls `disposeUploads()` (now also bumping an `epoch`), `federation.resetUnshown()`, and clears the rows and the banner. `resetUnshown` bumps the controller's own generation, terminates the parse worker through the bridge's existing `cancel()` (no leaked worker — `docs/TRAPS.md`), takes any models a waiting join already put in the scene back out, and forgets the store entries, keys, frame, offset and file coordinates the replaced load had fixed — otherwise the next file would have been streamed through the first one's project frame. Stale results are dropped by generation at every `await`: in `startUpload` (after the read and after the parse), in `prepare` (after the index and after the geometry), and in `addBatch` after its hold, which then returns `false` and commits nothing. After boot a pick still joins the federation, as designed, and `freeKey` now also counts keys still parsing and parsed-but-not-joined, so the same file twice there becomes `tiny (2)`. Two e2e cases prove it: the same file picked again mid-load, and two Recent pills in quick succession — no banner, and only the last pick loaded. **Superseded the same day for the after-boot half** — see the next row: a duplicate there is no longer `name (2)`. |
| **2026-09-24 — after boot, a pick whose model is already open or loading is confirmed, then replaces it** | Asked for by the user in these words, of the `name (2)` rule above: *"Dont do this. Just ask user to confirm then remove the old version."* **The question** is Electron's `dialog.showMessageBox`, attached to the window, over one new channel `file:confirmReplace` (`{ name }`, zod-validated in main; the preload imports the channel name only) — title `Replace model?`, message `"<file name>" is already open.`, detail `Replace it with the file you just picked?`, buttons `Replace` / `Cancel`, `defaultId` Replace, `cancelId` Cancel. It is **OS chrome, like the Open dialog**, not a designed surface: no element, class or copy of the design changes. It is asked only while `booted` (the landing page's fresh start is unchanged), once per file whose stem (`modelKeyOf`) is open or claimed by an upload, in the pipeline (`confirmPick`) before anything is queued. **Cancel** drops that file with no row and no warning; the rest of the pick continues. **Replace** loads the file under the **same key**, and the old model leaves only in `addBatch`, once the new one has parsed and right before it joins (`dropReplaced`): the scene half of `removeModel` (`unjoin`, now shared with it), plus by hand what the removal's own commit would prune — the old model's `hidden` ids, its colour override and the active flag — because the replacement can take the freed slot and so the very same ids. **Why not literally call `removeModel` first:** (1) replacing the only model would commit an **empty** federation — `booted` false, the landing page for a frame, the views reloaded and the assistant re-seeded on the next commit — and the next batch would count as a boot and reframe; (2) the replacement's store entry and worker file already sit under that key, and `removeModel` would delete them. The batch's one commit prunes selection, context menu, palette and colour-by as any commit does, and the SQL index is rebuilt once. **The session file** is now carried on the `BatchItem` and recorded at the join, so a session saved while the replacement parses still names the file on screen. **The worker** swaps the open file under a key only once the new file has parsed (the new one is closed if its index build throws), so a file that fails to parse leaves the old model *and* its raw lines exactly as they were; a later failure — the geometry stream — closes the new one, so a key never answers `get_entity_raw` from a file that is not on screen (the old model then answers "no raw line" until it is reopened). **A key still loading:** the newer pick wins. The older row goes at once and a parsed model waiting to join leaves `pending`, but a parse already running **cannot be cancelled on its own** — the one parse worker also holds every loaded model's file open, and `cancel()` terminates it — so it runs out, its result is discarded (`forget` closes its file), and the newer upload parses after it (`Claim.settled`), so two parses never share a key. **` (2)` survives in one place:** two files of one stem in a single pick (two Recent entries named `model.ifc` from different folders, "open all") — nothing of either is open and both were asked for, so the second is `model (2)` (`keysFor`). Verified: 15 unit cases (`tests/unit/replace-pick.test.ts`, the box stubbed) and one e2e case that stubs `dialog.showMessageBox` in the main process, asserts its options, and sees Replace leave one `tiny.ifc` after a second real parse and Cancel leave everything as it was. |
| **2026-09-24 — Help › About tells the owner's story** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"Under Help> About also add some short story about us creating this."* The item used to call `app.showAboutPanel()` with no options, so it showed Electron's defaults — and in dev the name `sgvue`, because `package.json` has no `productName` and nothing calls `app.setName`. **Now** `src/main/about.ts` (no `electron` import, so it is unit-tested) holds `ABOUT_STORY`, the story verbatim in one constant so a name can be added in one line, `ABOUT_NAME = 'SGVue'`, and two option builders. **macOS:** `app.setAboutPanelOptions({ applicationName, applicationVersion, version, credits: ABOUT_STORY })` — set at `installMenu` for the app menu's own `role: 'about'` and again on every Help › About — then the native panel. **Windows and Linux:** `dialog.showMessageBox(window, { type: 'info', title: 'About SGVue', message: 'SGVue <version>', detail: ABOUT_STORY, buttons: ['OK'] })`. **Why not Electron's panel on Windows:** it is a synchronous, unparented message box built from `applicationName` / `applicationVersion` / `copyright` / `credits`, which would show the text but blocks the main process while open and cannot be observed by the e2e; the message box is async, attached to the window, and is the same OS chrome the replace confirmation already uses. **Why not `app.setName('SGVue')`:** it would also move the dev `userData` folder. The version is `package.json`'s, built into main as `__APP_VERSION__` by the same electron-vite `define` the renderer uses, so About and the landing page read one source: `app.getVersion()` returns Electron's own `44.3.0` in a dev or e2e launch (`electron out/main/index.js`). **The story names the owner and the builder** — the owner asked the same day for their name and for Claude as the builder, and asked to be credited as Yong Yen — so `ABOUT_STORY` reads, verbatim: *"SGVue was made by Yong Yen, an architectural professional who wanted a faster way to look inside IFC models — to open a federation, walk its storeys and grids, and get straight answers about what is in it, without risking a single change to the files. Claude, by Anthropic, was the builder: together they turned a finished design into a desktop app, one careful step at a time. Everything stays on this machine; the model is only ever read, never written."* The e2e stubs the two calls in main, clicks the real menu item and checks the story arrived verbatim; the native box itself was not screenshotted. |
| **2026-09-24 — the landing page shows the app version** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"Show app version somewhere at homepage."* `v1.0.0` on the **left** of the strip that since 2026-09-21 holds only the theme toggle, style `font:400 11px/1 var(--mono);color:var(--faint)`; the strip's `justify-content:flex-end` becomes `space-between`, so the toggle lands on the same pixel. **The version reaches the renderer through an electron-vite `define`** (`__APP_VERSION__`, read from `package.json` when the config loads, declared in `src/renderer/env.d.ts`) — not a preload key, because the preload's key list is pinned by a test and a build-time constant cannot drift from the file it was built from. Measured against the previous build: the landing captures differ only in the version's own 144 pixels, plus 5 (dark) and 21 (light) single stray pixels in the backdrop grid. No new control. |
| **2026-09-24 — the sidebar's sections resize in height** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"The left hand side panel: Models, Storeys, Elements, Allow to resize them in height."* **Build:** two handles, `SplitHandle` in `Sidebar.tsx`, each a zero-height flex item on a section boundary (MODELS rows ↔ STOREYS header, STOREYS rows ↔ tree header) holding an absolutely placed 6 px `cursor:row-resize` hit area; its 1 px line is `transparent` at rest and `var(--accent)` under `.hv-split:hover` — the one hover rule not in the design. A drag uses pointer capture and sets the rows wrapper **above** the handle to `flex:0 1 <h>px;min-height:<one row + 10 px padding>;overflow:auto` (`sizedTail`), replacing that wrapper's >6-storey tail. The first drag **pins both lists** at their measured heights, because leaving the other one automatic made it jump: above six storeys the tree's 40 % floor would give way and the storey list would spring back to full height. The tree keeps `flex:1`; its floor is three rows (`TREE_MIN` = 116 px) once a list is sized — above six storeys, only once both are, so resetting one returns the 40 % rule. `clampSection` (pure, `state/selectors/sidebar.ts`, unit-tested) keeps a list at or above one row and lets it grow only by what the tree has above its three rows, so the aside never overflows at that window size; a later shorter window shrinks the sized lists toward their floors. Double-click a handle: that list back to `null`, the design's automatic layout. Sizes are store fields (`sideModels`, `sideStoreys`), session-only, never written to disk. The windowed tree (`GroupRows`) now also re-measures through a `ResizeObserver` on its scroller, since a drag changes its height without a window resize. **Parity:** undragged, the sidebar is pixel-identical to the previous build in both themes, and so is it after a drag and two double-clicks. |

Bubble position, size, style, occlusion and the overprint rule are untouched — **measured: the shown-bubble counts are identical before and after at every one of nine poses on three models**. **Chosen:** a bubble hidden by the overprint rule or by occlusion keeps its `BUBBLE_GAP × scale` of line, rather than the geometry being rewritten per frame from a DOM answer; in an elevation the extended end is where the lifted bubble's stem stands, so the two now join. |
| **2026-09-24 — dimensions between adjacent parallel gridlines, at each family's start end** | Asked for by the user in these words: *"Show the Dimension between gridlines also, offset from bubbles."* **Pairing** (`shared/annotate.ts`, `gridDimRuns`, pure, `tests/unit/grid-dims.test.ts`): the drawn lines of each family (the existing 10° grouping) sorted across the family along its `planNormal` **at each segment's midpoint** — a family admits lines 10° apart, and ordering by start end let a tilted member drop out of the middle of a chain — and each neighbouring pair dimensioned when their directions agree within **0.5°** (so a radial grid, one family by the 10° rule, gets none) and they are not coincident; two parallel lines recorded in opposite canonical senses (the ±90° flip) are handled. **Spacing** is Float64 from the segments, printed with `mmTxt`. **Where:** at the **start** end — the `−1` bubbles, `p0` — only. The run sits on the row of the *inner* of the pair's two start bubbles, so an axis-aligned family is one straight string, and a rotated grid clipped to the axis-aligned footprint steps with its bubbles instead of running through the building (checked at −43.4103°). It stands inward of that row by `gridDimStandoffPx`: the bubble's 13 px radius, 8 px, and the label's own half-extent **along the grid on screen** (its box measured once at build) — ≈ 32 px for a grid running up the screen, ≈ 54 px for one running across it; a first cut used a flat 34 px, which put a sideways family's 65 px labels on their own bubbles. The pixels become metres each drawn frame at the run's own midpoint, capped at the footprint pad so an end-on view cannot push the run into the model (`gridDimOffset`). That is why it is placed **before** `renderer.render` (`annotations.placeGridDims()`), not in `updateGridView` after it, which would draw it one frame behind the camera. **Look:** the selection dimension's own `materials.dim`, `DIM_TICK_FRACTION` of the stand-off for the ticks (drawn along the grid, so they lie on the gridline), and its label style; one `LineSegments` for all runs, in the grid group. **Visibility:** the labels join `declutterBubbles` with `after: true` — decided after every bubble, against every kept box in every row, so the bubbles' own answer is exactly what it was (unit-tested) — and are occlusion-tested like the bubbles; **a run is drawn only while its label is** (not crowded, not occluded), since a dimension without its number is noise, and **no run is drawn in an elevation**, where the bubbles lift over the roof. The sweep runs after the frame is drawn, so `annotations.gridDimsStale()` asks for one more frame whenever it changed a label whose run was drawn the other way; measured, the runs drawn equal the labels shown at every pose checked, and a still scene still draws nothing (`frame-triggers.cjs`). **Rays:** the occlusion sweep now fires one per dimension label too — **115** on the reference model (78 bubbles + 37 labels) in the iso and top poses, 24 in the north elevation where the dimensions are off — every one identical to the flat scan (`pick-parity.cjs`). **How many labels show** (137.9 MB reference model, 37 runs; guarded probe, plan view): **5 at the default framing** — 18 hidden by another label, 14 by a bubble — and **0 at 2.6× out**, the same before and after the stand-off change; zoomed onto the numbered row, 15 at 2× in, 12 at 3.3× (every other bay; 14 more off screen), 16 at 5× (16 off screen). The limit is arithmetic, not the rule: at the default framing a metre is **1.1 px**, so the file's 3.6–18 m bays are 4–20 px wide against a 50–65 px label, and a 12 m bay holds its label only from about 5× in. The drafting convention — show a label only where it fits between its own two gridlines — would show **0** at the default framing and at 2.6× out, so it was not applied; neither was a smaller label, which cannot close a 3× gap.
