The reference draws 1.75 and 1.5 jsx px a cell with box-shadows, which at any real ratio is fractional and blurs; scaling a 16 px canvas with `image-rendering` gives uneven cells at 150 %. So `veeCell(slot, ratio) = max(1, round(slot × ratio / 16))`, the backing store is `16 × cell` square, its CSS size is that ÷ ratio, and it is centred in the slot on a whole device pixel of it (`veeBox`). The sprite is therefore the nearest whole size to its slot — 16 px for all three at 100 %, 32 device px (21.33 CSS) for all three at 150 %, 48 / 32 / 32 at 200 % — and may stand a little outside the slot; **the slot is what the layout sees**, so no rectangle depends on the ratio. *(Amended the same day, at this step's review: **one number for every sprite — `veeCell(ratio) = max(1, round(ratio))`** — instead of one per slot. The per-slot rule gives the same sizes at 100, 150, 175 and 250 %; at 125 % it drew the header's sprite at 32 device px (25.6 CSS) beside a label's and the pill's at 16 (12.8), and at 200 % 48 beside 32. Under the one-size rule every sprite is 16, 16, 32, 32, 32, 48 and 48 device px at 1, 1.25, 1.5, 1.75, 2, 2.5 and 3; the slots, their margins and the centring are unchanged, so a sprite may now be smaller than its slot. `scripts/screenshot.cjs` asks the app for the rule (`window.__sgvueDev.vee.cell()`) instead of restating it.)* The CSS size is rounded **up** in its third decimal: Chromium truncates a length to 1/64 px, and `21.333px` at 1.5× is 31.98 device pixels, one short on an unlucky sub-pixel position. **Measured at real ratios** (page zoom, which to the renderer is display scaling, and with which `capturePage` returns real device pixels; device emulation does not — its PNG is a down-sample): at 1, 1.25, 1.5, 1.75, 2 and 2.5, in both themes, each sprite's captured pixels equal its canvas's backing store, 0 of 116 / 464 / 1 044 opaque pixels differing, fractional origins included; at 3 the header's and the pill's likewise (the label cannot be seen there: a 1900 × 1000 window at 3 is 633 × 333 CSS px and the log is squeezed away). *(Measured again under the one-size rule, the same way, at 1, 1.25, 1.5, 1.75, 2, 2.5 and 3 in both themes: all three sprites — the label's too, at 3 — 0 of 116 / 464 / 1 044 opaque pixels differing. `tests/parity/2026-10-01-vee/ratios-one-size/`.)* **The clock rule** (`app/Vee.tsx`). One `setInterval` at 125 ms for every sprite. It runs only while at least one sprite is mounted and the window is visible (`veeClockRuns`; `visibilitychange`), and not at all under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`, where every sprite holds clock frame 0 — which, with its own offset, is an open-eyed frame; frame 0 itself is the blink. **It never sets React state:** a subscriber computes its grid for the new frame, compares it as one string with the grid it last drew and repaints its own canvas only when it differs. Counted on the build: the pill alone, 7 s — 4 repaints (two blinks, each end) of 56 frames; three sprites — 12; the window minimised, 5 s — 0; pinned — 0; `frame-triggers.cjs` still reports 0 scene renders in 2 s at rest. *(Amended the same day: **the clock sleeps between changes.** It no longer ticks eight times a second. One timer is set for the next clock frame at which some mounted sprite's drawing differs — `veeNextWake`, a pure rule in `vee-grid.ts` whose per-state table is read off `veeGrid` itself over one 336-frame cycle, so it cannot drift from the drawing: `idle` at its blink's two edges (2 and 26 frames apart), `thinking` and `reading` every frame, `found` at its hop and its `!`, `done` at its bob's two edges. The frame is counted from wall time, so a late timer skips a frame rather than slipping; the delay is rounded up to a whole millisecond, because one cut short wakes a hair before its frame, finds nothing changed and wakes again (measured: 30 wakes where the rule says 26). Off while hidden, off under reduced motion, no React render, the dev pin — as before. **And every state but `idle` plays from its own first frame**, counted from when the sprite entered it (`veeSpriteFrame`): the reference feeds every sprite one global frame, so its half-second `found` shows whichever four of the hop's eight frames the clock happens to be on — sometimes none of the hop at all; counted from the state's start it is the hop itself, "one hop per finding" as the handoff's README has it. Held still — pinned, or under reduced motion — such a state shows the held frame, so frame 0 of the state. Counted on the build (`window.__sgvueDev.vee.wakes()`): the pill alone, 7 s — 4 wakes; three idle sprites — 12, where it was 56 ticks for the same 12 repaints; one sprite `thinking` — 8 a second; `reading` — 8; one `done` beside three idle — 26 in 7 s.)* A theme switch is heard on the attribute the tokens hang on — one `MutationObserver` on `data-theme`, not the store, because `setTheme` notifies the store before it writes the attribute — and a ratio change on a re-armed `(resolution: …dppx)` query, as the viewer does; both redraw every sprite once. Electron's device emulation changes the ratio without firing that query (measured), which is why the capture harness pins after it: `window.__sgvueDev.vee.pin(n)`, behind `DEVTOOLS`, holds the frame (`null` releases it); `paint` and `colours` there let the harness draw the five-state sheet with the app's own painter. **No two idle sprites blink together.** The reference offsets them `hexF + 5`, `+ 11`, `+ 17` (header, a reply's label, the pill) and has one past reply; a transcript has many, so a label's offset is `(11 + 4 × index) mod 28` — 11, 15, 19, 23, 27, 3, 7: a blink is two frames long and these are two or more apart from each other, from 5 and from 17. *(So: no two of the header's, the pill's and the first seven replies'. The offsets repeat from the eighth message, which then blinks with the first.)* **The light palette.** The owner designed dark only: `o` `--accent`, `b` `--step-bg`, `s` `--border-strong`, `e` `--ink`, `k` `--ground`, and two new constants, `h #8AF0E4` and `t #2B3B3A` — the unit test resolves the roles through `styles/design.css` and requires the handoff's `HPAL` exactly. Light keeps `o`, `b`, `s`, `e` on the light tokens and chooses three: **`k` = `--ink`** (the visor is the darkest thing on a light face, 13.2 : 1 against the body, as `--ground` is on a dark one); **`t` = `#CBD8D5`** (one step darker than the body at the dark facet's own contrast, 1.31 : 1 against 1.29 : 1, and between `--border` and `--border-strong` as the dark one is); **`h` = `#35C4B6`** (lighter than the light rim, 1.96 : 1, and darker than the page, 1.92 : 1 on `--step-bg` and 2.16 : 1 on `--card` — the value that balances the two, and the dark theme's own accent; 6.9 : 1 as the pip on the visor). A darker highlight was considered and rejected: it reads on the rim but not on the visor, which the brief asks to be clearly darker than the body. The weakest state is `found`, whose teal eyes are 1.92 : 1 on the pale body — it is carried by the `!` in `--ink` and the hop; and in `reading` the pip's side cells, `--border-strong` as the brief names them, are lighter than the pip on a dark visor where the dark theme's are dimmer. **Where it stands.** The sprite replaces the design's sparkle beside the title and on the pill (`Sparkle` is gone from `icons.tsx`) and precedes each reply's label (`ChatRow.hasSprite`, never the user's). The 16 × 16 grid is larger than the body in it — three empty rows above for the hop and the `!`, one below, two columns either side — so each slot overhangs its row and is pulled in by negative margins, as the reference's are: header `-4px -4px -1px -2px` (21 px, 15 px of flow — the sparkle's), label `-6px -4px -3px -5px` (19 px, 10 px of flow each way), pill `-3px -1px -1px -1px` (16 px, 14 px of flow — the sparkle's). The larger top margin is what puts the **body** on the row's centre line. **Measured** (mock, 1280 × 820, both themes alike, `tests/parity/2026-10-01-vee/README.md`): header `[939, 349, 328, 47]` and title `[974, 366.5, 249, 11]` before and after — the same box, so the same baseline; the first reply's label row `[951, 408, 81.41, 10]` → `[951, 408, 84.83, 10]`, 10 px tall before and after (2 px of growth were allowed); its bubble `[951, 422, 304, 54.75]` unmoved; the pill `[1201.42, 774, 66.58, 32]` → `[1179.09, 774, 88.91, 32]`, 22.33 px wider — its label's own width — at the same right edge, top, height, padding, gap and colours. Pixels differing from the build before in `vee-open`: 0 in the status bar, the header right of its title, the close button, the bubble, the log below it, the suggestions and the composer, the sidebar and the toolbar; 844 / 848 in the whole panel — the two sprites, the title's text and the name. **Tests:** `vee-grid.test.ts` (18 cases); `chat-panel.test.ts` — `authorOf`, `who`, `hasSprite`, the quote `sendChat` sends; `ai-prompt.test.ts` — the 29 lines and the name line; the smoke e2e — the title, the pill and a reply's label by their copy, a painted canvas sprite in each and none on a user row, the header 47 px, the label row 10 px and the pill 32 px tall — and a new case that watches the real clock: two pictures a sprite in four seconds, no two shut together, the rim recoloured by a theme switch, one picture each under an emulated `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`, and running again without it; the keyboard and Schedules specs by the new copy. The evaluation suite still grades — oracle 36 / 36, null 0 / 36, neither calling the API. |
| **2026-10-01 — the bottom row declares its height as a lane, `--brow`, which the chat panel and the property card clear** | The row's own entry (above) recorded this as not solved and as a decision rather than a fix: the chat panel stops `bottom:52px` above the stage's foot (`SGVue.dc.html:431`) and the property card 56 px (`top:184px`, `max-height:calc(100% - 240px)`), which is room for the row's one line and not for a centre zone two levels tall — the reset pill over a hint, a wrapped hint, the centre placed above the bars. With an element selected, the panel open, a tool hint and something hidden, the panel lay over the whole of the reset pill at the default window; in a 900 px window it covered the pill and the top of the hint. It is taken now, together with the wider Ask pill (below), which makes the centre stand above the bars at 900 px too — where the same panel would otherwise cover it. **The rule, pure:** `browFrom(centreTop)` in `selectors/lanes.ts`, where `centreTop` is the measured distance from the stage's foot up to the top of the centre zone (0 with nothing in it). `0` while `centreTop ≤ 52` (`ROW_ROOM`, the panel's own `bottom`): a one-line hint alone is 46.8 px up and the reset pill alone 49.5, and that is the design's own layout, 2.5 px under the panel's line as the design draws it — nothing may move there. Otherwise `ceil(centreTop + 6 − 52)`: what it takes to keep the design's 6 px (`ROW_CLEAR`, the panel above the Ask pill: 52 − 14 − 32) above the zone, rounded up so that what takes the lane stays on whole pixels — so the clearance is 6 px and under 7. 88.3 px (the pill over a one-line hint) → 43; 110.5 (the pill alone above both bars) → 65; 149.3 → 104; 166.09 (760 px, the pill over a two-line hint above the bars) → 121. **The plumbing:** `BottomRow.fit` — which already runs after every render of the row and on every resize of the row or a zone — reads `stage.bottom − centre.top` and writes the store's `brow` through `setBrow`, which writes only on a change; `App.tsx` declares `--brow:${n}px` on the stage beside `--rlane` and `--abar`. Three consumers: the chat panel's `bottom:calc(52px + var(--brow, 0px))` and `max-height:calc(100% - ${cardTop}px - 64px - var(--brow, 0px))`; `clampChatSize`'s height bound (a sixth argument, default 0), so a corner drag stops where the CSS would; and the property card's `max-height:calc(100% - 240px - var(--brow, 0px))`. **Nothing in the row reads the lane**, and neither consumer is a zone of it, so publishing it cannot change what was measured: the row's rectangles are the same with the panel and the card open as without (asserted in the e2e case). A change of placement renders the row again and that render measures again, so when the cause is a state change the lane is right before the next paint, and after a window resize it follows in the one frame the row itself already takes. A store field rather than `style.setProperty` on the stage, because React owns that element's style and the resize clamp needs the number. **With the lane at `0px` every computed style is what it was** — the panel `bottom: 52px`, `max-height: calc(100% - 130px)`; the card `max-height: calc(100% - 240px)`: read before and after, the same strings, and with the pill alone at 1280 × 820 and 1264 × 755 the panel and the card have the rectangles they had. **The Ask pill is wider.** `Ask Vee` beside the sprite is 88.91 px (66.58 while it read `Ask`). The row's rule is unchanged and takes the width it is given: at the default window only the Ask pill moves, and the laser's 522 px hint still has room for one line (544 px between the zones at 1 264 px, 566 before). **At 900 px the centre now stands above the bars**: they leave it 576 − 286.91 − 88.91 − 20 = 180.2 px, less than the reset pill is wide (182.73) and than `HINT_MIN` (200); with the narrower pill there were 202.5, the pill stood between the zones and the laser's hint wrapped to three lines there. And the status bar and the Ask pill fit side by side at their own offsets down to a 400 px stage (a 700 px window; 378 and 678 before). **Measured** (mock, both themes alike; `tests/parity/2026-10-01-vee/README.md` § 4 has every rectangle): no two of the row's five pieces intersect at 1280 × 820, 1264 × 755, 900 × 700 or 760 × 700, at rest, with the reset pill alone, or with the laser tool, a full action bar and the pill; the status bar is where it was in all twelve. With an element selected, the panel open, the laser's hint and a storey hidden: `--brow` `43px`, panel `[606, 348, 330, 420]` → `[606, 305, 330, 420]`, card `[948, 184, 320, 580]` → `[948, 184, 320, 537]` at 1280 × 820, the panel 6.7 px above the pill; at 900 × 700 `104px`, panel `[312, 124, 244, 420]`, card 356 px tall; at 760 × 700 `121px`, panel `[176, 113, 240, 414]`, card 339. None of the five meets the panel or the card at any of the four sizes, where 1, 2, 4 and 5 pairs did. **Not solved, by scope:** the lane is the centre zone's, not the bars' — with the row one line, the panel in the card's lane still lies 6 px across the padded end of a full action bar at the default window, as it did before the row; and the colour legend does not take it, so where the centre stands above the bars it is still where a legend is. In a window too short for both, the lane takes from the panel and the card rather than from the row. **Tests:** `selectors.test.ts` — `browFrom` at each measured height, the 6-to-7 px clearance for every value, `brow()`; `chat-panel.test.ts` — the clamp with a lane, without one, short of the bound, and under the 240 px floor; the smoke e2e's bottom-row case — `--brow` is `0px` at rest and with the pill alone, equals `ceil(foot − pill top + 6 − 52)` with the laser's hint, and with a selection and the panel open nothing among the five, the panel and the card meets, the panel is 6 to 7 px above the pill, both computed styles are the design's plus the lane, and the row's own boxes have not moved; its narrow step is 715 px now, and at 900 px the centre is asserted above the bars. |
| **2026-10-01 — the assistant's reply is drawn while its turn runs: a pure core fed by the turn's real events, a little store, one painter, and a clock that can be pinned** | The second step of the owner's "Ask Vee" handoff (`design-reference/ask-vee/`, the specification; its px ÷ 1.5 are app px). The handoff is a 16 s scripted video on invented numbers — `Reply`, `Piece` and `hexState` in `ask-thinking-hex.jsx`, pure functions of a time `T`. The app has to play the same choreography from whatever a real turn does, so it is built the way the reference is written. **The core** (`src/renderer/ai/`; no DOM, no React, no clock of its own): `trace-facts.ts` — what one executed tool call contributes, from its name, its **resolved** input and the federation; `trace.ts` — `traceReduce` folds the turn's events (`begin`, `tool_start`, `tool_exec`, `done`, `fail`) into a **timeline** (when each happened, and the facts each call brought), `traceCues` derives the cue times, and `traceFrame(timeline, T, layout)` returns everything visible at `T`: Vee's state, the status and its roll, the ticker's lines and counts, the bubble's height, every cell (place, size, colour role, opacity, scale, ring), the beam, each word's and each row's opacity. `traceSend` is the same for what stands outside the reply: the lift, the user's row, the send button. The three curves (`enter` easeOutQuart, `glide` easeInOutCubic, `pop` easeOutBack), every duration, stagger and offset are the reference's; its sizes are its px ÷ 1.5 (`J`). **Checked against the handoff itself.** `tests/unit/trace-fixtures.ts` cuts `MOTION` … `beamAt`, `hexState` and the easing functions out of the jsx and runs them. The reference's scenario is rebuilt as real elements (4 models of 140 / 244 / 20 / 8, its own 86 walls among the first 140, its 9 flagged ones carrying no `FireRating`) and as a real turn — Send, a `tool_start` at its Read, the call's data 0.4 s later, `done` at its Answer, and nothing else told to the reducer — and `traceFrame` has to draw what the jsx draws at 330 instants between Send and the end of its hold: all 412 cells' place, size, scale, opacity, colour and ring (over 20 000 comparisons), the bubble's height, the ticker's three lines and counts, the beam, Vee's state through each of the nine findings, the words and the rows, the lift and the button. **Three knowing differences from the reference's code**, each tested. During Read the reference gives its 86 future walls no flash — its script knows what the question will filter to; the app cannot, so every cell flashes, which is what the handoff's README describes. The reference's last ring is thrown 0.52 s before its Answer and takes 0.6 s, so 0.08 s of it — under a ten-thousandth of its opacity — runs on into the answer; the port finishes everything at the answer. And the status's seconds are the turn's own: `8s` for the reference's 8.2 s, where its label is the literal `7s`. **The cues, from real events.** Send is `chatBegin`. Think is Send + 1 s, the reference's Send phase. Read is the turn's first `tool_start` — or its first execution, should that come first — and never before Think + 0.4 s. Each later step starts when its data exists and the step before it has had its designed time (Read 1.8 s, Filter 1.6 s, Check 2.4 s, a note 0.6 s); calls that pile up behind a step are not replayed one by one — when the step ends the ticker goes to the latest of them. Answer is `done`. **The store** (`ai/trace-store.ts`): the one live timeline, the index its reply has in the transcript, and the clock — **outside the shell store**, because a turn delivers a handful of events and its view repaints sixty times a second, and neither is something every `ShellState` consumer should hear about. `ai/bridge.ts` feeds it where it already handles the turn: `sendChat` → `begin`; the stream's `tool_start`; `done`, whose summary (`ChatTrace`: the status, the rows, whether the bubble is the trace's full-width one) goes onto the committed `ChatMessage.trace`; `aborted` / `error` → `fail`. `executeTool` reports each call through `ToolContext.trace` **after `resolveNames`**, so a property is spelled as the file spells it, and before the executor runs, so a slow tool is narrated while it works. Only a turn the panel shows has that callback: a harness's throwaway call, or a call for a turn that is no longer running, is not narrated. The design's `chatStage` (`:1336`) is gone from the store. **The view** (`app/Trace.tsx`, `app/ChatPanel.tsx`): one `requestAnimationFrame` loop with one painter. The painter is called with the trace's time and says whether anything is still moving — `true`: the next frame; a number: the time of the next cue, which a timer waits for (reduced motion); `false`: nothing — and it is also called directly from the panel's layout effect after every render, so what React has just laid out is never painted as it stands. The matrix, the beam and the rings are one canvas, in device pixels; text is DOM, moved through inline styles the painter owns. A node the trace drives gets `transition-property:none`: the design's reduced-motion rule (`*{transition-duration:.01ms !important}`) otherwise holds each write for one more frame, which is the one thing a state shown "at its end" must not do. **The live reply and the committed reply are the same element.** The transcript is one keyed list and the row being written has the key its message will get; the e2e marks the row, its sprite's canvas and its matrix canvas before `done` and finds the marks on them afterwards. **A reply the trace leaves is finished at once.** If the next turn begins while a reply is still arriving, the painter lets go of that row (`releaseParts`) and it is simply the reply as React laid it out; the e2e sends the next question a tenth of a second after an answer and requires every word in, every row in, the bubble at its own height, the status one plain line, the mascot `idle` and the rows' cells at rest — at once, and unchanged a second later. (With the release taken out, that test fails on a reply frozen 52 px tall with its words at opacity 0.) **The clock is injectable.** Every event is stamped with `traceNow()` and the painter asks the same function. `window.__sgvueDev.trace.pin(T)` (behind `DEVTOOLS`) holds it, and `chat.step` — `begin`, `start`, `exec`, `done`, `fail`: `scriptedTurn` in `ai/bridge.ts`, which `chat.turn` now runs straight through — steps a turn without the API. So `scripts/screenshot.cjs` holds one turn at the eight instants of the handoff's stills, the same on every run, and `scripts/lib/parity-states.cjs` holds `chat-busy` three seconds in. **Reduced motion:** `layout.reduced` makes every animation a step at its cue — each state at its end, and nothing between two cues (tested: seventeen samples between each pair of cues are one and the same frame) — and the painter sleeps until `traceNextCue`. **Nothing existing grew:** the live reply's label row is 10 px, as every label row is; the status is the row's own 10 px type with a 13.3 px window laid over it for the roll, which does not make the row any taller. **The e2e has no API and no dev harness** — the suite runs against the production bundle, which has no `window.__sgvueDev`. A turn is a conversation between main and the renderer, so `tests/e2e/vee-trace.spec.ts` replaces main's two turn handlers (`ai:turn:start`, `ai:turn:abort`) from the test, as the About test replaces `dialog.showMessageBox`, and then sends the events itself over the real channels; everything on the renderer's side — `sendChat`, `handleEvent`, the real executor on the demo building, the trace, the panel — is the app's own. The page samples itself every 25 ms so the test can ask what was shown, in what order. Reading a canvas back moves it from the GPU to the CPU, and the two round a 7 px square's corners differently — the same 24 `roundRect` calls come out as two byte-different pictures — so a picture is compared by which pixels are at least a quarter there and which solid colours are among them, not by its bytes. Tests: `trace.test.ts` (40), `trace-facts.test.ts` (16), `marks.test.ts` (12), `trace-wiring.test.ts` (14: a real `sendChat`, the events main forwards, a tool run through the real executor), `vee-trace.spec.ts` (5, in the built app). |
| **2026-10-01 — a rule is *scope* or *check*, and that split is what the trace's Filter and Check are** | The reference shows three steps — read the model, filter to walls, check a property — because it is a script. The assistant has no such steps: it calls one tool with one rule list. The split that recovers them (`ai/trace-facts.ts`): a **scope rule** is a rule on one of the five built-in properties that say *which* elements a question is about — `Model`, `IfcEntity`, `PredefinedType`, `ObjectType`, `Level` (`SCOPE_PROPS`); a **check rule** is every other rule — a property key, `Name`, `Material` — which is what is *asked of* them. It applies to any executed tool whose input has a top-level `rules` array with a live rule (`matchFn` returns a predicate), OR group by OR group, as `ruleGroups` in `shared/ai-schema.ts` splits them. **The scope set** is the elements matching the rule list with its check rules taken out (`scopeRules`); an OR group left with no rule is satisfied by every element and OR binds looser than AND, so the scope is then the whole federation. **The matched set** is `matchFn(rules)` over every element — the executor's own predicate, so the trace cannot disagree with the answer. *Filter* — `Filtering {what}`: the distinct `IfcEntity =` values in the federation's own spelling, two at most, else `N classes`; with no such rule, the first scope rule's value, with its operator when that is not `=` (`≠ L1`, `~ wall`, `no Level`). Its count is the scope's size, falling from the element count. Its unit is the class as a plural noun (`IfcWall` → `walls`, `IfcBuildingElementProxy` → `building element proxies`, `IfcElementAssembly` → `element assemblies`) only when every OR group names one class outright with `=` and all name the same one; otherwise `elements`. *Check* — `Checking {props}`: the property names prettified as the property card prettifies them (`prettyKey`), two at most, else `N properties`. Its count is the matched size, rising as the beam passes each flagged cell; its unit is `missing` when every check rule is `absent`, else `found`; the number turns `--accent-ink` once it is above 0. With no scope rule the beam scans the read grid. A later rule-bearing call restarts from the read layout — no wave — and plays its own steps. A tool with no live top-level rule is a **note**: its `TOOL_STAGE` phrase, capitalised, no count, the matrix unchanged (`set_filter_stack` carries its rules inside `steps`, so it is one). **One departure from the brief, to keep the numbers true.** The brief has a Filter whenever a scope rule is present. Built, a call shows one only when its scope really narrows: `IfcEntity = IfcWall OR FireRating is empty` has a scope rule and a scope of everything, and "Filtering IfcWall · 412 walls" would be false twice over. **The status** a finished turn rolls to comes from its **last** rule-bearing call: `checked {scope count} {noun} · {s}s` after a Check (`checked 412 elements` when there was no Filter), `found {count} {noun} · {s}s` after a Filter alone, else `{s}s` — whole seconds from Send to `done`, to the nearest, at least 1; a count is grouped as the boot audit groups its own (`2,767`). **`TOOL_STAGE` gained two phrases** — `find_properties`, `find_nearby` — because the fallback, `running <tool name>`, would put an internal name in front of the user; a test now requires a phrase for every tool in the catalogue. `FIRST_STAGE` and `stageFor`, the busy row's, are gone. On the demo building, "which walls have no thermal transmittance?" is 412 elements → 80 walls → 24 missing, on five storeys. |
| **2026-10-01 — the matrix is proportional, and exact where it fits: one cell per element while the grid has room, else `ceil(count / capacity)` to a cell — on whole device pixels** | The reference draws 412 squares of 2.13 px at a 2.7 px pitch across its 286 px bubble — 103 columns of 4, grouped by model with a 2.67 px gap; then 86 of 4.67 px at 6.65 px in 2 × 43; then 6.67 px cells at 9.33 px in the rows. A real federation has any number of elements and the panel can be dragged down to 240 px (192 px inside the bubble), so the layout is computed from the bubble's inner width (`planRead`, `planRules`, `rowLayout` in `ai/trace.ts`). **Read grid:** 4 rows, the cells grouped by model in the federation's own order, a gap between two models (given up when the gaps alone would take more than a quarter of the width). One cell per element while the columns fit; otherwise each cell stands for `ceil(count / capacity)` elements — and one more for as long as the columns every model keeps for itself still do not fit, because **every model keeps at least one column**. **Filter grid:** 2 rows of the larger cells, one per scope element when that fits, else bucketed the same way; each filter cell glides from the read cell that holds its first element, and the read cells that give none shrink away. **Check:** a cell is flagged when it holds a matched element, and the ticker's count rises by that cell's matched elements as the beam passes it — so the count ends on the exact matched size, whatever a cell stands for. **A number never buckets:** every count on screen is the federation's own. Tested on 100 000 elements in three models, one of them of seven, at the reference's width: 244 to a cell, each model with a column of its own; its 2 767 walls in 84 cells of 33; the count ending on the exact 395; eight rows under the answer, the eighth `+5 more levels`. **Whole device pixels** (`traceLattice(dpr)`). The reference's 2.13 px squares at a 2.7 px pitch are not a pixel grid at 100 % scaling, and the brief asks that cells land on device-pixel boundaries. Every pitch, size, gap and origin is the reference's ÷ 1.5 **rounded to whole device pixels for the display's ratio**, with at least one device pixel between two cells in both directions. At 150 % — the handoff's own scale — pitch / size are 4 / 3 (read), 10 / 7 (filter) and 14 / 10 (rows): its numbers, rounded. At 100 %: 3 / 2, 7 / 5, 9 / 7. At 125 %: 3 / 2, 8 / 6, 12 / 8. At 200 %: 5 / 4, 13 / 9, 19 / 13. A cell at rest is therefore a crisp square on every display — a unit test walks seven ratios, three widths and four states and requires every x, y and size to be whole — and a square under 5 device px is drawn with square corners, since a radius would only blur it. With no ratio (`dpr: null`) the lattice is the reference's numbers untouched, and that is what the unit test compares with the jsx. **What it costs at 100 %.** The app's bubble is 282 px inside (the reference's tracing: 286), and a 2 px cell needs a 3 px column, so one to a cell the demo building's 412 elements would need 318 px. They are drawn **two to a cell: 206 cells in 53 columns (18 + 31 + 3 + 1), 167 px of the 282** — which is what the brief's own rule says for a grid that does not fit. At 125, 150 and 200 % every element has a cell and the grid spans the bubble as it does in the reference (at 150 % the 103 columns need exactly the 423 device px there are). With the reference's unrounded 2.7 px pitch the app's 282 px would hold 101 columns — two to a cell there too — so the cause is the panel's width against the tracing's, not the rounding. The 80 walls fit the filter grid one each at 100 % (2 × 40 across 278 px). **The colours.** Dark is the handoff's: a cell at rest `--border-strong`, a flagged one `--accent`, and three greys of its cell loop that have no token — the wave's and the beam's flash `#7E9693`, a scope cell waiting for the beam `#405351`, a checked and unflagged one `#2D3B3A`. The handoff is dark only; light keeps each grey at the same place between the same neighbours (the flash 46 % of the way from the cell to `--ink`, the waiting cell 16 % from the cell to the flash, the checked one 65 % from the bubble to the cell): `#748382`, `#B3C3C0`, `#CFDCD9`. A unit test resolves both palettes through `styles/design.css`, requires the handoff's five dark values exactly and each fraction within 0.05 in both themes. |
| **2026-10-01 — the answer is never delayed by animation, nothing animates for ever, and the trace is only narration** | The reference's steps have designed durations (Read 1.8 s, Filter 1.6 s, Check 2.4 s) and its answer comes when its script says. A real answer comes when the model is done — often while the trace is still showing an earlier step, sometimes before `Thinking` has come up at all. **The rule:** when `done` arrives, whatever is queued or in flight is finished at that instant and the Answer choreography starts there. In `traceFrame` every animation of the trace is `pre(cue, …)`, which reads as complete from the answer on; the scene the matrix shows becomes the turn's last rule-bearing call at its end — whether its steps were ever shown or not — so the flagged cells fly from where they would have ended; the ticker's last line is the last step with its final count, fading; and Think and Read are clamped to the answer. Tested: a `done` half a second into the Filter (an instant before: the walls mid-glide and nothing checked; at the answer: the nine findings lit in their places, 77 dimmed, the ticker on `Checking Fire Rating · 9`, no beam, and the answer's own offsets counted from there); a `done` 0.3 s into a turn, before `Thinking` has even come up (the bubble open at 52 px, settled two seconds later); a turn whose tools all ran within milliseconds of its Send (`chat.turn`). **Calls that queue up are not replayed.** If three calls' data exists when a step ends, the ticker goes to the latest: it shows what it can honestly show, and the trace never falls behind the turn by more than a step. **Nothing animates for ever.** The frame loop runs from Send and stops when the painter says nothing moves — `traceEnd`, never more than 1.95 s after the answer: the words take at most 0.35 + 1.6 s (a long reply's 65 ms stagger is shortened so that the whole reveal fits), the flyers 0.5 + 0.7 + 0.75 s (their 50 ms stagger shortened the same way once there are more than fifteen), eight rows 0.95 + 0.56 + 0.4 s. Between turns there is no frame and no timer. With the panel closed the painter finds no log and stops; a reopened panel draws the turn as it stands, because a frame is a function of the time and nothing else. A turn the store no longer shows as running — its transcript replaced under it by a new federation's boot audit — is not drawn at all, where it would otherwise wait frame after frame for an answer to a row that is not there. Under reduced motion there is no loop: one frame a cue. **Measured** (`scripts/frame-triggers.cjs`, a turn stepped in real time on the mock): about 410 (one per display frame) trace frames and 0 scene renders in the 6.8 s the turn ran, 119 and 0 in the 2.2 s of the answer, 0 and 0 in the 2 s after it, the loop idle. The e2e, in the built app: no `data-moving` on the log, one unchanging matrix and one unchanging reply, two seconds after the answer. **The sprite clock is the one exception, under its own rule** (the Vee row, amended): it sleeps between the frames at which a mounted sprite's drawing changes. **The trace is narration.** `narrated()` in `ai/bridge.ts` wraps everything the bridge tells the trace, and the painter's frame is wrapped as well: a failure is logged, the trace is dropped — the reply then arrives as a plain one — or lets go of its row, and the tool still answers, the turn still commits and the window is still there. The painter runs in a layout effect and the renderer has no error boundary, so without that a fault in a decoration would blank the app; and `executeTool` calls the trace before the executor, so without it a fault there would cost the model its tool result. Tested with a federation the trace cannot read and the executor can: the call answers `ok`, one `[trace]` error is logged, the timeline is gone, and `done` commits a reply with no `trace` on it. |
| **2026-10-01 — the rows under an answer: one per storey that holds a match, eight at most, twelve cells at most, each a button** | The reference ends on four rows — `L1 ▪▪▪ 3 walls` and three more — with the flagged cells flying out of the matrix into them. Built from the turn's **last rule-bearing call**, when it matched at least one element and the turn produced **no `table`**: a table already lists the result, and its rows select too. One row per storey holding a match, in `federation.storeys` order; a storey name the ladder does not carry follows in the order it is met, and elements on no storey come last, under the design's em dash (`storeyRows`). **At most eight** (`ROW_CAP`): beyond that the eighth is `+{k} more levels`, with what they hold summed (`capRows`). A row is the storey's name (`--muted`, mono 10 px), its cells, and `{n} {noun}` on the right (`n` in `--ink`, the noun in `--faint`, singular for one), 26 ÷ 1.5 px tall, under a 1 px `--border` rule. **Cells:** one per element while the largest row has no more than twelve (`ROW_CELLS`), else each stands for `ceil(largest / 12)`. A row cell's flyer leaves from the matrix cell that holds its first element, and a flagged matrix cell that starts no row cell flies to the row cell holding one of its own, so nothing is left behind (x on `glide`, y on `enter`, so the path curves; 0.75 s each, 50 ms apart — less once there are more than fifteen, so that the last has landed 1.95 s after the answer). A Filter with no Check flies too, its cells turning the accent on the way. **Layout** (`rowLayout`): the cells start where the reference starts them (44 ÷ 1.5 px from the text's edge) unless the longest storey name needs more — `Roof` moves them to 33 px on the demo building, a long name such as `Level 02 - Garden Roof Deck` further; the count column is as wide as its longest text; a name too long for six cells and the count to fit beside it is cut with an ellipsis and carries its whole text as a native `title` (the 2026-10-01 clipped-text rule). In a narrow panel a row holds fewer than twelve cells, and they stand for more. **A row is a button:** a click selects its elements through `select(ids, false)` — the chips' own action — and the ids are kept on the message with the status (`ChatMessage.trace`: at most eight rows). **Its hover is `hv-card`, not the brief's `hv-step`.** The bubble is `--step-bg`, which is exactly what `.hv-step` paints, so that hover would change nothing at all; `.hv-card` is the design's other row hover and reads on the bubble in both themes. **At rest** a finished reply's cells are painted by its own bubble (`rowCells` — tested equal, as a picture, to the settled frame's) and painted again when its box, the theme or the display's ratio changes, so an old reply re-wraps and resizes with the panel like any other; its rows still select. **Measured** on the demo building (ratio 1): `L1 5 walls`, `L2 5`, `L3 5`, `L4 5`, `Roof 4` — 24 cells of 7 px at a 9 px pitch; the rows `[962, 545.25, 274, 17.33]` to `[962, 614.56, 274, 17.33]`, the rule `[962, 541.25, 274, 1]`, the bubble `[951, 490.5, 296, 148.05]`. The e2e clicks `Roof` and then `L1` and reads `4 selected` and `5 selected` on the property card. |
| **2026-10-01 — two inline marks in a reply, rendered as elements: `**bold**` and `` `code` ``** | The handoff's answer has a bold lead (`9 of 86 walls`) and a model's name in the mono face, and the assistant already writes both marks. `ai/marks.ts` — pure, tested — splits a reply into pieces of three kinds (plain, bold, mono) and into words (`markWords`), and `app/Trace.tsx` renders each piece as a React element: bold is `font-weight:600`, code the mono face at 11 px (the handoff's 16.5 ÷ 1.5) in the text's own colour. **Nothing else is markup, and nothing is ever HTML.** There is no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` and no parser to feed: an angle bracket or an entity in a reply is text (tested with an `<img onerror>` and a `<script>`). A mark with no partner prints as it was written; bold cannot begin or end on white space, so `2 ** 3 ** 4` is arithmetic; nothing between two marks is nothing marked; code inside bold is mono; nothing nests inside code; a single asterisk and an underscore are not marks. **Linear in the reply's length — it runs on the answer path.** `done` counts the reply's words (`traceDone`, called from `handleEvent`) before the reply is committed, and the bubble splits it again to render it, so what the split costs must not depend on what the reply holds. An opening therefore has exactly **one** candidate for its partner — the next mark of its kind: bold that would begin with white space is decided before any search, and if the next mark cannot close the opening (nothing between them, or white space before it) the opening is literal and nothing later is tried. One search an opening. The first version looked past a candidate that could not close, and review found it quadratic: on `**a **a **a …` every opening walked every later mark and failed each for the same reason — measured, 0.12 s for 10 kB, 0.44 s for 20 kB, 1.7 s for 40 kB, **44 s for 200 kB**, twice over (the count, then the render), with the window frozen and the answer waiting behind it. Now 200 kB of `**a ` splits in 4 ms and its words are counted in 9 ms; 200 kB of `** ` in 3 ms; 200 000 bare backticks in 3 ms; 420 kB marked properly in 5 ms. A unit test holds each under 100 ms. What the rule gives up: `**a ** b**` — where CommonMark passes over the middle pair and closes on the last — prints as written. **`Words` is memoised on the text**, so a reply is split and laid out once, not at every keystroke in the composer (the panel renders again at each one). **Why words.** The reveal fades each word in on its own — 65 ms apart, 0.36 s each, rising 7 ÷ 1.5 px; a long reply's stagger is shortened so that the whole reveal takes at most 1.6 s — so a word is a `position:relative` inline span holding its pieces: still inline, so the text wraps exactly where plain text would, and a word that starts plain and ends bold (`(**12**)`) is one word. Any run of white space is one gap, as it is in the plain bubble, whose `white-space` is the default. Only a reply that came from a turn is rendered this way; the boot audit and a user's message are plain text, as before. The model is not told about the marks: it writes them already, and a reply that uses others — a list, a heading — shows their characters, as it did before. |
| **2026-10-01 — the chat panel has a stop control, and `You` stands on the left: two things the port deliberately did not have** | Both are the owner's handoff (`design-reference/ask-vee/README.md`, "Interactions & behaviour"), which is the specification for the assistant's panel since the Vee row. The 2026-09-18 row — "the chat panel gets no stop control" — recorded that the design has none; it is amended, not contradicted: what it refused was an addition the design did not ask for. **The stop control** is the send button itself. While a turn runs its arrow cross-fades to a square — the handoff's 12 ÷ 1.5 = 8 px, radius 2 px, `--accent-ink`; `--ground` under the pointer, where the button's own hover fills it with `--accent` — its `data-tip` and `aria-label` read `Stop`, and a click calls `abortChat()`, which has been in `ai/bridge.ts` since Phase 9a for the acceptance script and the gateway's 120 s cap and now has a designed surface. The square stands for as long as the store says a turn runs — also in the one case with no trace to draw, a trace that failed. **A double-click on Stop stops once and sends nothing.** The button decides per click, so the second click of a double-click landed either while the turn was still running — and asked main to stop a second time — or just after main's `aborted` had ended it, when it would have sent whatever had been drafted in the composer meanwhile. For 300 ms after it has stopped a turn the button does nothing at all (`sendClick` and `STOP_GUARD_MS`, a pure rule in `selectors/chat.ts`); Enter is not held, since Enter never stops anything. Unit-tested both ways round, and the e2e's stop is a double-click: main is asked to stop once, the transcript has one question, the draft is still in the composer — with the guard taken out that test fails on two stops. Main answers `aborted`; the bridge ends the trace and calls `chatFail('Stopped.')`, so the transcript is left exactly as a failed turn always left it: the question stays, the reply being written and its trace are gone, `Stopped.` shows. **What did not change:** Enter during a turn still does nothing — the re-entry guard is still the `chatBusy` test in `sendChat`; nothing is `disabled` — the composer can be typed in while a turn runs, and what was typed is still there to send afterwards; and closing the panel still stops nothing. **`You` on the left:** `chatRow` gives a user's row `flex-start` and the assistant's corner (`11px 11px 11px 3px`; the design: `flex-end` and `11px 11px 3px 11px`, `:2015`); its `--sel-bg`, its 86 % and its label are unchanged. It is what makes the lift a straight line: the composer's text and a bubble's text both stand 11 px inside the log's content edge. **The send.** The button presses (0.9 → 1 on `pop`; the handoff's `#1B4A45` written as `--sel-bg` 18 % toward `--accent`, so it follows the theme); the typed line lifts — one line laid over the panel, at the width the text will have in its bubble so that it wraps as it will, from the composer's baseline to the bubble's in 0.7 s on `glide`, at one x; the bubble comes in at 0.94 → 1 about the middle of its left edge; `You` fades in; the placeholder returns (`--ph` on the input and one rule in `styles/vee.css`, since a pseudo-element has no inline style). **The busy row is gone** with what only it used: `BrandBusy`, the `ifctrace`, `ifcbreathe` and `ifcdot` keyframes, `chatStage` and `setChatStage`. `TOOL_STAGE` stays, as the words of a note step. **Not shipped**, as the handoff's README says they are the video's: its camera moves, its background dimming and the Minimal / Wide variants; and the composer keeps the app's own focus ring rather than the reference's border animation. **Accessibility:** the reply being written is `aria-hidden` — the matrix, the beam and the ticker are decoration — and one visually hidden line in the log says `Vee is working on it.` while a turn runs; the committed reply is announced by the log's own `aria-live`, as before. e2e: the button's label and tip before, during and after a turn; Enter during a turn leaves the second question in the composer, unsent; the square is 8 px with a 2 px radius at opacity 1 over an arrow at 0; a click asks main to stop exactly that turn, `Stopped.` shows, the live row and its canvas are gone, and Enter then sends what was typed. **Measured** (mock, 1280 × 820): the user's bubble `[951, 499.75, 261.44, 54.75]` with its text at x 962, the lifting line at x 962 for the whole 220 px of its way; the stop square `[1235, 735, 8, 8]` in the button `[1223, 723, 32, 32]`. |
| **2026-10-01 — a tooltip inside the chat log is generated only while it shows, and the log's overflow is vertical only** | Step 1 measured a defect and left it: the chat log shows a horizontal scrollbar as soon as it scrolls vertically — `scrollWidth` 348 against a `clientWidth` of 320 with the parity transcript, the same on the build before Vee. **No child was wider than the log.** The box that crossed its right edge was a pseudo-element. The design's tooltip (`[data-tip]::after`, `design.css`) is always laid out, `opacity:0` until its control is hovered, centred under the control — and the one under the design's right-aligned `reply` of a user's row (123.6 px wide under a 37 px button that ends at the content's edge) reached past the log, as a chip's at the end of a line can. An invisible box still counts as scrollable overflow. **Fixed at the cause** (`styles/vee.css`): `[data-role='chatlog'] [data-tip]:not(:hover):not(:focus-visible)::after { content: none }` — inside the log, a tooltip that is not showing is not generated, so it is no content at all. `@starting-style` gives it the state it fades in from, which the always-present box had for free, so the design's 0.1 s fade still runs. The log is the only scroll container that carries `data-tip`s; every other tooltip in the app is untouched. Belt and braces, the log's `overflow:auto` is now `overflow-x:hidden; overflow-y:auto`: a tooltip that *is* showing near the edge cannot bring the scrollbar for as long as it shows, and is clipped at that edge as it always was at the other three. Moving a user's row to the left (the row above) takes its `reply` away from the edge as well. **Measured:** `scrollWidth` = `clientWidth` — 328 before the log scrolls, 320 once it does. The e2e asserts it on a transcript that scrolls, reads the tooltip's `content` as `none` at rest and as `"Reply to this message"` at opacity 1 under the pointer, and `scrollLeft` 0 afterwards. The trace adds nothing wider than the log: its canvas is its bubble's size, inside the bubble's own `overflow:hidden`. |
| **2026-10-02 — parity with the user, phase 1 of 4: the reversible view state the assistant could not reach is reachable, each through the action its own control calls** | The owner's direction of 2026-10-01 (the row "the assistant should be able to do whatever the user can do in the app"): *"assistant should possess everything user can do on the app. that would be the best case scenario."* A read-only audit set every user action beside the assistant's tools and sorted the gaps into three classes — A, reversible view state; B, an effect outside the view (files, the clipboard, anything not undoable), which needs a consent rule first; C, the user's alone (the API key, the model, Quit) — and into four phases. **This is phase 1: class A's switches, selection, history and models, the defects the audit found in the existing tools, and reading back what the assistant can set** (the rows below). Not in it, and not started: the camera, viewpoints, markups (phase 2), the consent gate and class B (phase 3), and the Schedules window's own controls and placing a markup (phase 4). **What became reachable** (`shared/tool-schemas.ts`, `ai/executors/view.ts`, `ai/executors/interface.ts`): (1) `toggle_display` gains `groundGrid` — the canvas grid, through the toolbar button's own `setGroundGrid`; recorded on 2026-09-24 as *not* reachable by the assistant, and that record is amended in place — `snap` (`toggleSnap`) and `originalMaterials` (`toggleNative`). (2) `select_elements` gains `mode`: `replace` (the default, and all it did before), `add`, `remove`, `clear` — a tree click, a Ctrl-click and Esc, each through the store's one `select`. `add` and `remove` hand `select` the whole resulting list in **one** call: the store's own `'toggle'` mode takes one id, and two thousand of those would be two thousand `setSelected` calls. `zoom` defaults to true for `replace` only — a Ctrl-click never moves the camera. Every result carries `selectionCount`, what is selected once the call returns. (3) `apply_visibility` gains the actions `undo` and `redo`: the store's `step(back)`, so the redo branch, the 50-deep cap and the pickability that follows `active` are the store's. The result carries `historyBefore` and `history` (`canUndo`, `canRedo`); with nothing to step to it says so and changes nothing. (4) `color_models` takes `null` for one key — `setModelColor(key, null)`, the palette's own `reset`, which leaves Original materials as it was; the empty map still clears every override and turns Original materials back on. The result carries `originalMaterials`, so the model knows what a cleared override now shows as. (5) Two new view tools, in a group of their own (`PARITY_TOOLS`, between the read-only additions and the Schedules window's, so the catalogue test names each group's members exactly): **`set_models`** `{visible[]}` — the eye beside each model; `visible` names the models to leave showing and `[]` all of them (`set_storeys`' own shape, so a call states the whole result and is idempotent by construction), written as **one `up({ modelVis })`**, the call `toggleModel` itself makes, rather than a toggle per model — one request is one undo step, and the patch the scope guard holds back is the patch that would have been written; and **`set_interface`** — any subset of `theme`, `units`, `treeMode`, `sidebar`, `card` (the six cards, or `none`), `tool`, `search` (≤ 200 characters) and `schedulesWindow:"open"`, through `setTheme`, `setUnits`, `setTreeMode`, `togglePanel`, `openCard` / `closeCard`, `setTool`, `setSearch` and the toolbar button's own `window.sgvue.openSchedules()` (the one handler here that is not a store action; `make_schedule` and `export_schedule` already call it). Its enums are the store's own unions spelled out in `shared/` (`INTERFACE_*`), and a unit test fails `tsc` if a card or a tool is added to one and not the other. Its description says it changes how the app is set up, never what is in the model or what is visible, and is for when the user asks. **Unchanged rules, all still enforced:** every entry is `read` or `view`; strict schemas carry no length keyword (bounds are in the description and in zod — `MAX_MODEL_KEYS` 200, `INTERFACE_SEARCH_MAX` 200); no tool takes a path; **no new IPC channel and no new preload key**. **Cost, measured** (`JSON.stringify(apiTools())` and `contractText()`, UTF-8 bytes): the tools block **32 049 → 36 885 B** (31 875 → 36 667 characters; 31 → 33 tools), the contract **16 597 → 17 790 B** — 6 029 B more of cached prefix, about 1 500 tokens, written once and read at the cache price after; the catalogue change rebuilds the cache once. **Tested:** `ai-parity.test.ts` (new, 53) — every extension and both tools through `executeTool` on the mock with a recording stub viewer: the state a call leaves is the state the click leaves, and the viewer hears of it from the action; `ai-tools.test.ts` (+10) — the two tools, their schemas, bounds and enums, the read-only-additions assertion adjusted for the new group and saying so; the evaluation suite's ten new dry-run cases. |
| **2026-10-02 — a tool that wraps a toggle compares first and calls the control's action only when the state differs; a setting already as asked is reported, never flipped** | Half the controls behind the tools are toggles — `toggleGrids`, `toggleLevels`, `toggleDims`, `toggleSnap`, `toggleNative`, `togglePanel`, `openCard` (the open card's own button closes it), `activate` (the active model's own target leaves activate mode) — and a tool is asked for a *state*, not a click. **The rule:** read the state now (through `ctx.state()`, never a snapshot taken before an earlier setting in the same call moved), and call the action only when it differs. So every call is idempotent — the same call twice changes nothing the second time: no store write (the tests compare the state object's identity), no viewer call, no undo entry, no ↺ — and a setting already as asked is said to be, in `already` beside `changed`. **Three defects this fixes.** (1) **`activate_model` with the key that is already active** handed that key straight to `activate`, which toggled activate mode **off**, under the reply "Activated X."; the active key now stays active and says so, and `key:null` with nothing active changes nothing rather than pushing an undo entry for a no-op. Leaving activate mode is still `key:null`, which makes the pill's own call (`activate(active)`). (2) **`toggle_display`'s `dims`** said nothing at all when it was already where it was asked to be, while the other three were listed; all seven are now in exactly one of `changed` and `already`, the message reads `grids off, levels already off.`, and `setShadows` is no longer called for a shadow that is already so. `changed` used to list the unchanged ones too; it is what moved, now. (3) **`manage_filters`' `enable` / `disable`** on a step already in that state wrote the stack again and pushed an undo entry; it says `Step 2 is already enabled — nothing changed.` **`set_interface` does not mark the turn as having changed the view** (`ui.acted`): the per-turn ↺ restores the five `VIS_KEYS`, and could not put a theme, a unit or an armed tool back, so it is not offered for one. The audit found the same on turns that only toggled a display switch or moved a section — a ↺ that restores nothing — and widening the per-turn revert to everything the assistant can set is phase 2's, because it changes a designed control and needs the owner's word. *(Amended the same day, phase 2: the owner gave it — the revert row below — and `set_interface` now marks the turn for every setting but the Schedules window, which no revert closes.)* **Tested:** for every one of the seven switches, the eight interface settings, `set_models`, `color_models`, `activate_model`, `select_elements`' `add` / `remove` / `clear` and `manage_filters`' `enable` / `disable`: the second identical call leaves `useShell.getState()` the very same object and makes no viewer call. |
| **2026-10-02 — read-back is sparse per turn and full on demand** | The larger hole the audit found: the assistant could **set** several things it could not **read**. Neither the view state sent with each turn nor `get_view_state` said whether gridlines, levels, shadows, dimensions or snap were on, where a section plane stood, which side it kept or whether it cut, what a model was coloured, or whether anything could be undone — so "is the section actually cutting?" had no answer, and a view left in an odd state by one turn was invisible to the next. Two places could carry it, and they are paid for very differently: the per-turn view state rides on **every** turn and stays in the transcript afterwards; a tool result is paid for once, when asked. **The rule: sparse per turn, full on demand.** *Per turn* (`shared/ai-schema.ts`, `sparseViewState`): the eleven fields a turn has always been sent are always there, and four more appear **only while something is not at its default** — `display`, holding just the switches that differ from the store's first values (`DISPLAY_DEFAULTS`: gridlines, shadows, the canvas grid, snap and original materials on; levels and dimensions off — a unit test holds the constant to the store); `tool`, when it is not `select`; `modelsHidden`, the keys of loaded models whose eye is off; `sectionPlanes`, for a set plane whichever of `offsetMm`, `flip` and `cut` is not what the Section card's chip writes (`planeDefaults`, checked against `planePatch`: 0 mm for a gridline, 1 200 for a level, not flipped, cutting). A view at its defaults adds nothing — not a key, not a byte — and one instruction line tells the model that whatever is not listed is at its default. **Measured on the mock** (`'Current view state: ' + JSON`): at boot **258 B → 258 B**; with a filter step, both cuts at their defaults and a selection **363 B → 363 B**; with all seven switches, the tool, a model and a plane off their defaults at once **363 B → 595 B** (before, none of it was said). *On demand* (`executors/read.ts`, `get_view_state`): everything in full, at its default or not — `display` (the seven switches, named and ordered as `toggle_display` takes them); `sectionPlanes` (`grid` and `level`: `{name, offsetMm, flip, cut}` or `null`), with **`section` kept the one string it was**, because the evaluation suite grades it; `history` (`canUndo`, `canRedo`); `models` (each loaded model's `key`, `name` as its sidebar row shows it, `visible`, `color` override or `null`, `active`); and `interface` (`theme`, `units`, `treeMode`, `sidebar`, `card`, `tool`, `search`, and `schedulesWindow` — whether that window is open, the one thing `set_interface` sets that nothing else reported). It is built on `viewStateCore`, not on the per-turn state, so no sparse field is in it twice under another shape. Its existing fields are unchanged but for `modelsHidden`, which now lists loaded models only, in the federation's order (the eye map can still carry a key for a model since unloaded). On the mock: **357 B → 1 034 B** at boot, 486 → 1 256 B in the working state. **Bounded:** at most fifty models (`VIEW_MODELS_CAP`, with `modelsTotal` and `modelsTruncated` when cut), a model's name clipped at 120 characters, the search text at 200. **File text stays out of what is cached and out of what rides on every turn.** A model's key is in the cached schema already; its *name* is its file's (or a library's label) and is new here, so it is in `get_view_state`'s result only — never per turn, never in `system[1]`. **The structural check** (`tests/unit/ai-readback.test.ts`): `SessionSource` (`shared/session-codec.ts`) is the app's own list of what a review's state is — what a session and a share link save. The test takes its keys at run time (`sessionPatch` builds one) and requires each to be either **reported** — the test changes that key and that key alone, and `get_view_state` must read differently — or **excluded by name with its reason**. Two are excluded, and a fourth test shows the exclusion is true rather than forgotten: `coords`, the base point (changing it rewrites every coordinate read-out and has no reset, so it waits for phase 3's consent rule; `get_model_info` reports the file's own georeferencing), and `hlColor`, the last highlight colour pushed to the renderer (a step's own colour is phase 2). A key added to the session without a decision there fails the build. *(Amended the same day, phase 2: a highlight step's `color` is reported, so `hlColor` is no longer excluded — `coords` is the one exclusion left — and the per-turn `modelsHidden` is capped at twenty keys, with `modelsHiddenMore` counting the rest.)* |
| **2026-10-02 — everything new that can hide runs `scopeCheck`; two old ways round it are closed; an undo that would hide is refused in words, not held** | The design's guard (`SGVue.dc.html:1412`, `:1464`): a change that would leave nothing visible is refused, one that would leave under 5 % waits behind the panel's Apply button with the **patch** it would write, and a change that cannot hide (`highlight`, `show`) is exempt. Phase 1 adds three things that can hide, and the audit found two existing paths that never ran it. **`set_models`** runs it on the `{ modelVis }` it would write: blank is refused, under 5 % is held with that patch (Apply hands it to `up()`, which leaves exactly what the eyes leave — tested against three `toggleModel` clicks), and a call that only brings models back is exempt, as `show` is. **`manage_filters`** — `enable` and `apply_set` wrote their stacks unchecked, so a step could be switched on, or a saved set recalled, that blanked the view with no Apply button; they now go through one helper, `guardStack`, on the patch `updStep` / `applyFilterSet` would hand `up()` (enabling a `highlight` step is exempt, as it is in `apply_visibility`). **`remove` and `move` were named unguarded too, and they are exempt — not by choice but by arithmetic:** `visFn` is a conjunction of the enabled isolate / hide steps (`shared/rules.ts`), so taking a step away can only reveal more and changing their order changes nothing. They are passed through `guardStack` with that exemption stated at the call, so the four operations are decided in one place; a literal check would have refused `remove` in a view that is already blank for another reason and held a reorder behind Apply in a view the user had already confirmed, for a change that hides nothing. *(Amended the same day, phase 2, after review: a call that could only ever answer "go ahead" was ceremony, so `remove` and `move` no longer pass through `guardStack` at all — a one-line comment at each points at the 216-stack test — and a `move` to the position the step already has is no move: no write, no undo entry.)* The proof is a test: over all 216 stacks of three built from six rules, for every step and every move, the visible set after a `remove` contains the set before and after a `move` equals it. `disable` and `clear` can only reveal as well, and were never in question. **`undo` and `redo`** can hide — undoing a "show all" brings an isolate back. The store's history had no reader but `step`, so one was added: `peek(back)` on `UndoHistory` (`shared/undo.ts`) and `peekHistory(back)` beside `historyDepth` in `state/shell.ts` — the snapshot a step would restore, moving nothing; **the only thing added to the store for the whole phase**. The executor computes what the step would leave and whether it takes anything out of view; a step that only reveals is exempt. One that hides and would leave nothing, or under 5 %, is **not taken and says so, in words** — *"That undo would leave only 3 of 412 elements visible, which needs the user's own click, so it was not done. The user can press Undo in the action bar themselves"* — rather than held behind Apply. Holding it is not possible yet without lying about what Apply does: `applyPending` hands a **patch** to `up()`, which is a new change on top of the history (it pushes an undo entry and drops the redo branch) and does not re-apply pickability for a restored `active`; an undo is a move *through* the history. A pending **action** beside a pending patch is phase 3's mechanism, and this refusal becomes a held action then. **The option rejected** was to exempt undo altogether, on the ground that it only returns the view to a state that already existed: that state may have existed only because the user confirmed it with their own click, and text in a file that reached the assistant as an instruction could then walk the view back to it with no click at all — the guard is the one place such a call is stopped, so a tool that can hide is not let past it on an argument about history. The user's own ⌘Z is not guarded, as it never was. **Not changed, and noted:** `set_storeys` writes `storeyVis` with no scope check — the design's own tool has none — so one storey with a handful of elements, or none, can still be soloed without a click. It was outside this phase's list. **Tested** (`ai-parity.test.ts`): each refusal and each held patch with its exact message, the state object unchanged and the history depth unmoved; each held patch applied through `up()` against the control's own action; the exemptions (a model brought back in an under-5 % view, a highlight step enabled in one, an undo that only reveals, `remove` and `move`). |
| **2026-10-02 — a turn holds one change behind Apply, and a second is refused in words** | The panel shows one pending row a reply, and `applyUi` wrote `turn.pending = ui.pending` — so a second change the guard held in the same turn **replaced the first in silence**. The model had been told twice that the user had an Apply button; the user was shown the last. With more tools that can be held (the row above), it would happen more often. `oneHeld(outcome, turn)` in `executors/index.ts`, between the executor and `applyUi`: when a turn already has a pending change and an outcome brings another, the outcome's `forModel` is replaced with what is true — *"A change is already waiting for the user's Apply button from earlier in this turn (<label>). Only one change can wait at a time, so this one was not held and nothing was changed…"*, `applied:false`, `pending:false`, `alreadyWaiting` — and its patch is dropped; the first keeps the slot, the very same entry. Nothing has to be undone: an executor that holds a change returns before any store action. Whatever else the call handed the panel (chips, a table) is kept, a change that needs no confirmation still applies while one is waiting, and the next turn holds one again. A pending row from an earlier turn is its own message's and is not affected. **Tested** across three tools as the second, with the exact message; `oneHeld` itself as a pure function. |
| **2026-10-02 — two instruction lines, a ticker that reads the call, and ten evaluation cases; the grader sees what the assistant can now move** | **The prompt** (`main/ai/prompt.ts`): two additions, in sentence case with dated comments, after the filter-sets line and before the turn budget — 31 lines, none of the design's removed or reworded. *One:* `toggle_display` also switches the canvas grid, snap and original materials, and `set_interface` changes how the app itself is set up; none of these changes the model or which elements are visible, and they are for when the user asks for that setting, never a step towards answering something else. *Two:* the per-turn view state names a switch, the armed tool, a hidden model or a section's offset, side and preview only when it is not at its default, so whatever it does not list is at its default — and the line states those defaults — while `get_view_state` reports all of it in full. The second line is what makes the sparse rule safe: without it a missing field would read as unknown rather than as default. The selection modes, undo and the model eye are not in the prompt; their tool descriptions carry them. **The ticker** (`ai/stages.ts`, `ai/trace-facts.ts`): the thinking trace narrates a call with no rules as a note, in the tool's one phrase — and two tools now do things that phrase would misstate. `toolPhrase(name, input)` reads the call: `select_elements` with `mode:"clear"` is `Clearing the selection` (not `Selecting elements`), with `add` / `remove` and no rules `Changing the selection`; `apply_visibility` with `undo` / `redo` is `Undoing the last change` / `Redoing the last change`. `set_models` is `Showing and hiding models`, `set_interface` `Adjusting the interface`. It only ever compares a field with a string and looks a phrase up as an *own* property, so no input — `null`, an array, a tool called `constructor` — can make it throw; the trace's own guard is not relied on. **The evaluation suite** (no API): ten cases with `oracle.calls` — `ans-display-readback`, `op-canvas-grid`, `op-clear-selection`, `op-add-to-selection`, `op-undo` (a step back that can be redone, so a reset does not pass), `op-hide-model` (the model's own eye, so a filter step does not pass), `op-model-colour-reset`, `op-light-theme`, `op-arm-laser`, `op-active-stays` (the defect: its view check alone would pass an agent that did nothing, so the reply has to say it) — 46 in all. They carry no `requires`: they were written with the capability, not ahead of it. `gradeView` gains checks for `display`, `interface`, `history`, `modelsHidden` and `modelColours`, each failing — never passing — on an observation that does not carry the field. **`VIEW_FINGERPRINT` now includes the display switches and the hidden models**, so an answer-only case that turned the canvas grid off or hid a model fails `view-unchanged`; before, nothing in it could tell. **`MUTATING_NAME` is made consistent with the catalogue guard** (`isMutatingName`): `export_schedule`, the one owner-approved tool whose name says "export", is exempt by that exact name — until now any turn that called it would have failed `no_write`; `export_model` is still a write. And the `ref-export` oracle no longer says "there is no export tool", which stopped being true on 2026-09-28: the refusal is that nothing writes model data. **Measured, through the suite's own runner into a scratch folder, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` unset:** oracle **46 / 46** (answer 6/6, tabulate 5/5, analyse 7/7, operate 21/21, refuse 4/4, resist 3/3), null **0 / 46**. No live run was made. |
| **2026-10-02 — parity with the user, phase 2 of 4: the camera, saved viewpoints, markups and one filter step in place, each through the action its own control calls** | The second phase of the owner's direction of 2026-10-01 (*"assistant should possess everything user can do on the app"*): what the audit listed as class A under the camera, the Viewpoints card, the Markups card and the Filter card's step controls. Not in it, and not started: every deletion and everything with an effect outside the view (phase 3, behind a consent rule), the Schedules window's own controls and placing a markup (phase 4). **What became reachable** (`shared/tool-schemas.ts`, `ai/executors/view.ts`, `ai/executors/saved.ts`): (1) **`set_view`** gains `fit` (`extents` or `selection`), `azimuth`, `elevation` and `zoom`; the row below has the convention. The controls that move the camera are not store actions at all — a double-click, F, a drag and the view cube live in the viewer — so **two actions were added to the store** rather than have a tool reach behind it: `fitView(what, zoom?)`, which is `viewer.zoomExtents(zoom)` (the viewer's own answer to a double-click on empty space) or `viewer.zoomTo(selIds, zoom)` (F, and the context menu's Zoom to — for everything selected, not only the element picked last); and `aimCamera(azimuth?, elevation?)`, which is `viewer.lookAlong(θ, φ)` — **the one method added to the viewer**: `cam.setAngles`, a flight, `invalidate()`. `zoomExtents` and `zoomTo` take an optional factor, applied by `fitBox(box, zoom)` after the fit and stopped where a dolly stops (`0.4 × near` and `900 × scale` in perspective, `0.15` and `400` for the orthographic half-height). The projection goes through the persp / ortho button's own `toggleProj`, compared first. **Three defects of the old tool went with the rewrite:** it compared the requested projection with the one read *before* the view was set, so `view:"north", projection:"persp"` from a perspective view left an orthographic elevation under a reply that said `(persp)` — an explicit projection now wins; it reached into the viewer for the projection behind the store; and it marked every call as a change, where it now says `acted` only when the pose, the lit view or the projection is different afterwards. (2) **`manage_views`** `{op, name?, number?, to?}` — the Viewpoints card: `list`, `save` (the card's "Save current view", `saveView`; with a `name`, the double-click that renames the new row, `renameView`), `restore` (a click on the row, `restoreView`, which pushes undo exactly as the click does) and `rename`. (3) **`manage_markups`** `{op, name?}` — the Markups card, read: `list` returns its two lists as the card shows them, `focus` is the tag that zooms to one (`focusPoint`). (4) **`manage_filters`' `update`** and **`set_filter_stack`'s `color`** — one step changed where it stands (its own row below). Both new tools are in `PARITY_TOOLS`, **35 tools** in all. **Unchanged rules, all still enforced:** every entry is `read` or `view`; no tool name carries a mutating verb (`save`, `rename` and `update` are `op` values, which the guard does not read as names); strict schemas carry no length or numeric keyword — the bounds are in the descriptions and in zod (`azimuth` ±360, `elevation` ±90, `zoom` 0.1–20, a viewpoint's name ≤ 80 characters, a lookup name ≤ 200, a markup's name ≤ 20); no tool takes a path; **no new IPC channel and no new preload key**; nothing here deletes, clears or places anything. **Cost, measured** (`JSON.stringify(apiTools())` and `contractText()`, UTF-8 bytes): the tools block **36 885 → 42 798 B** (36 667 → 42 506 characters; 33 → 35 tools — `set_view` 2 070 B, `manage_views` 1 712, `manage_markups` 989, `manage_filters` 2 514), the contract **17 790 → 19 098 B**: 7 221 B more of cached prefix, about 1 800 tokens, written once and read at the cache price after; the catalogue change rebuilds the cache once. **Tested:** `ai-parity-2.test.ts` (new, 49) — every new input and operation through `executeTool` on the mock, with a stub viewer whose camera is the **real rig** (`tests/unit/rig-viewer.ts`; `viewer/camera.ts` touches no renderer, canvas or DOM), so what is asserted about the camera is the pose it would fly to; `ai-tools.test.ts` (+9) — the schemas, bounds and enums; the evaluation suite's ten new dry-run cases; three camera triggers in `scripts/frame-triggers.cjs` (31 of 31 repaint, 0 scene renders at rest). |
| **2026-10-02 — the camera's direction is two angles in the project frame: azimuth, the bearing it looks towards, and elevation, how far it looks down** | The view cube offers 26 directions and a drag every one; the tool had six names. A direction has to be something the model can say and read back, and the same thing in both: **`azimuth`** is the compass bearing the camera **looks towards**, in degrees clockwise from **project north** — the model's own +Y, which is not true north and is said not to be — so 0 looks north, 90 east, 180 south, 270 west; **`elevation`** is how far it looks **down** from level: 0 level, 90 straight down, −90 straight up. Bearing-and-tilt is how a person says it ("from the south-west, looking down a little"); the alternative, the rig's own spherical angles, are where the camera *stands* measured anticlockwise from +X, and a model handed those would get east and west backwards half the time. **The arithmetic** (`shared/view-angles.ts`, pure, no three.js): the rig holds `θ`, `φ` with the camera at `target + dist × (sin φ cos θ, sin φ sin θ, cos φ)`, Z up, so `θ = −π/2 − azimuth` (wrapped to (−π, π]) and `φ = π/2 − elevation`, clamped to the rig's own pole distance (`POLE_PHI` = 0.0001 rad — `VIEWS.top`'s value, moved here with the table so the app and the description cannot disagree). **In these terms the six view buttons are** south 0 / 0, west 90 / 0, north 180 / 0, east 270 / 0, top 0 / 90 and iso 315 / 29.8, and `set_view`'s description lists them from the same constant (`VIEW_DIRECTIONS`) — **to the tenth of a degree the read-back is rounded to, so that the numbers go both ways**: a camera on a named view reads back exactly the description's pair, and that pair, sent back as a direction, is recognised as that view. (`iso` was first quoted as 315 / 30; the rig's is 29.84°, 30 is 0.003 rad off it against a tolerance of 0.001, and a model that sent the description's own numbers was told `view: null` — found in review, the same day.) A test holds each of the six both ways, and lands each, asked for as azimuth and elevation, on the camera the button gives. **What a call does.** A direction turns the camera **where it stands** — about what it is looking at, at its present distance — as a drag does, and lights no view button (`view: null`, as a click on the cube leaves it); an angle left out keeps the camera's own; at a pole the bearing is kept, so `azimuth:90, elevation:90` is a plan with east up the screen. With `fit` it also frames, which is a click on the cube. `fit` alone frames without turning; `zoom` is a factor on the fit and alone fits the building. A named view together with a direction is **refused in words** and nothing moves; so is `fit:"selection"` with nothing selected. **Order, and why:** the view, then the projection, then the direction, then the fit. The projection is swapped before any framing because the rig converts distance to half-height on a swap, which is not the fit's own arithmetic: a fit taken first came out a tenth looser in ortho, a tenth tighter in perspective, and different again when the same call was repeated. Every call without a named view therefore lands on the same camera when repeated. A named view is the toolbar's button, and **the design's button pressed twice from a perspective view reframes by that same tenth** (the swap sizes the first press, the fit the second); that is the reference's rig, it is kept, and a test says so rather than hide it. **Read-back** (`cameraBrief`, `shared/ai-schema.ts`): `camera { view, projection, azimuthDeg, elevationDeg }`, angles to one decimal, `view` the named view the camera is *actually standing on* (within 0.001 rad) or `null` — in `get_view_state` always; per turn only while the camera is **not** standing on the view the `view` field names, so a camera left by a view button adds nothing and the instruction line that states the defaults says so.
