No ring is added: only the design's own `:focus-visible` rule can draw one. The user's own confirms and prompts keep the focus they had, to the character. **Rejected:** putting the focus on Cancel (a stray key would then answer *no* to a question the user has not read — harmless, but it takes the question away); inferring it from the label. The tool brings the window forward, sets the turn's one request (`ui.asked`), and is never told what the user chose — the `exportAck` pattern. **A save that forgets.** Phase 3 found that a `save_set` could forget a saved filter set, and the brief said to look for the same here. It is here: the window's Save replaces the setup the schedule was loaded from, and at the cap of a hundred the oldest is pushed off the end; a duplicate at the cap pushes one off too (`forgottenBySave`, `forgottenByDuplicate`, `schedule-ui/schedules.ts` — `saveSchedule` writes exactly the plan they read). The user's own Save does both without a question, by ifcTable's rule, and is unchanged. One asked for from chat raises that window's confirm first (`Save over the saved template “…”? What it holds now is replaced, and cannot be brought back.`), is held with `ask: false` when the turn has already asked for something, and at the click saves only while a save would still forget exactly what the question named. A save that only adds is done at once. A question raised for the assistant quotes a saved setup's name with control characters and direction marks taken out (the main window's `labelText`, for the same reason), and `make_schedule`'s `title` — which becomes a saved setup's name when the schedule is saved — is refused when it holds one. **Not asked:** `load` and `apply_template` replace the open schedule without the click's "Replace the current schedule setup?" — as `make_schedule` has since 2026-09-28: the replacement is one step on that window's undo history, which the tool can itself undo. **While one of that window's dialogs is up** everything that would change it answers `busy`; the two lists are still answered. **Rejected:** sending a definition for `load` and saving from the main window (the saved setups are that window's `localStorage`; a second copy of the save rule would drift); a `delete` behind the chat's own Apply (the deletion lives in another renderer, and a row here would have needed a message that performs it — the thing the gate exists to not have); awaiting the dialog (a person can take longer over one than a tool's budget, and the tool must not learn the answer). `save` writes that window's `localStorage`, as its Save button does — it is not a fourth disk writer. | **2026-10-02 — a markup is placed by the viewer's own commit for a click, at a point that is named: an element's bounding box, or a project-frame point** | The user places a spot coordinate or a laser measurement by clicking a surface; the assistant could neither. **Chosen.** `manage_markups` gains `place_spot` and `place_measure`. A tool has no pointer, so the point is named: an element (`id`) and `at` — `top` (the default), `centre` or `base`: the middle of its **bounding box's** top face, of the box, or of its underside (`shared/annotate.ts`, `boxPlace`, pure) — or an explicit `point {x, y, z}` in project-frame metres, the frame `get_element` reports a box in. **The very same markup a click makes.** In `viewer-core.ts` the click handler's two lines became `commitSpot` and `commitLaser`, and two new `Viewer` methods, `placeSpot(p)` and `placeMeasure(p, normal, selfId)`, call the same two functions: the record, its labels, its number in the Markups card and its id come from `annotations.addSpot` / `addLaser`, as for a click, and a measurement's rays are `laserFrom`'s — the same picker, the same section clipping, the same rule that a ray is not fired into the surface it stands on. The store's `placeSpot` / `placeMeasure` take the project-frame point and subtract the scene's whole-metre `offset`. From `top` and `base` the laser is handed the face's normal (+Z, −Z) and the element as the surface it stands on; from `centre` and from a point, neither — the middle of a box is on no surface, and its rays read the element's own faces (a wall's thickness, length and height). `placeMeasure` says whether anything was placed: when no ray reads anything `addLaser` adds nothing, as for such a click, and the tool says so. **It is the box, and the tool says so** — in its description, in every result (`on: "bounding box"`, *at the middle of the top face of the bounding box of "…"*) and in the prompt line: the box is axis-aligned and conservative, so on a sloped or L-shaped element its middle can be off the real surface. **`show`** sets a spot tag's two states (its level alone, or its full E / N / Z) by the tag's own toggle (`annotations.ts`, `setExpanded`, which the tag's click calls too), for one spot or all, idempotently. **Bounds:** at most fifty of a kind by a tool — what one result can list — and a point within ±10⁷ m. **The revert** (amended the same day — the follow-up). Phase 4 left a placed markup standing — *an addition to the user's own list, as a saved viewpoint is* — and its result said *this reply's revert does not remove it*. That was the wrong side of the line. A spot or a measurement drawn in the 3D view is the most visible thing a reply can add, and markups are **session view state**, not a saved list; the owner's rule for `revert` is that it puts back everything that reply changed. So the line is: session view state is put back; saved lists — viewpoints, filter sets, saved schedule setups — are not. The two markup lists are still not a *part* — no session carries them, so they are in no snapshot — and the removal is by id: `place_spot` / `place_measure` report the new record's own id, the one the viewer gave it, read off the list it grew (`ui.placed` → `TurnState.placed` → `ChatMessage.placed`), and the store's `revertTurn` removes exactly those that **still exist**, through `dropMeasure` / `dropSpot` — the removal the Markups card's × runs — in the same click as the rest of the revert. A markup the user placed, or another reply placed, stays; one the user has already removed is a no-op with nothing said; a reply that only placed one is offered `revert`, because it changed something that can be put back; a reverted reply gives the record up as it gives up `undoSnap`, so a second revert does nothing. **A turn that is stopped, or fails, after it placed a spot or a measurement leaves that markup in place**: no reply is committed, so there is no `revert` to take it away — as every other change a stopped turn made stays — and the user removes it with the Markups card's ×. **Nothing under `renderer/ai/` names a removal** — the guard's list of performers has `dropMeasure`, `dropSpot`, `clearMeasures` and `clearSpots` — so a tool still cannot delete a markup: the executor only reports what it placed, and the user's click on `revert` is what takes it back. A spot tag's state set by `show` is still not put back. Deleting and clearing stay gated. **Rejected:** building the record in the store (an imitation — the labels, the numbering and the dispose path are the annotation layer's); a screen coordinate to pick at (the model has no view of the screen, and the answer would depend on the camera); snapping to a corner (the snap is the pointer's); and — in phase 4 — making placed markups revertable by dropping them, which the follow-up chose after all: the click on `revert` is the user's own, and it removes only what that reply itself placed. Tests: `annotate.test.ts` (`boxPlace`, and the laser's directions for each normal), `annotation-store.test.ts` (project − offset; the viewer's answer passed through), `ai-parity-4.test.ts` (ten: each place, the frame, a base point, `selfId`, nothing placed, the cap, the refusals, `show`, and the gated delete of a placed one — and eight on the revert: placed, then gone by id through the card's own ×; offered on a reply that only placed one; not offered where nothing was placed, or for a spot tag's state; the user's own and an earlier reply's left where they are; one already removed, a silent no-op; a second revert, nothing; beside a visibility change, one click; a saved viewpoint kept), `assistant-markups.spec.ts` in the built app (the spot's tag reads the box's top to the millimetre; the laser reads real lengths; both are in the Markups card; and the revert, measured: with a spot placed by hand and then one by a reply — action bar `1 measures · 2 spots`, two tags, card rows C1 and C2 — one click on that reply's `revert` leaves `1 measures · 1 spots`, one tag and the row C1, the hand-placed spot's tag and row and the earlier reply's M1 as they were) and four rows of `frame-triggers.cjs`. |

Phase 3 measured that this app refuses that API always — main denies every permission request, by design — so the control copied nothing, raised an unhandled `NotAllowedError`, and, having no flash, said nothing either way. **Chosen.** The button calls the store's `copyTable(table)`, which goes through `copyText` (`renderer/clipboard.ts`: the async API, else a selected off-screen field and `execCommand('copy')`, which works under the user's activation — and a click on the button is one). **No flash**: the design gives this control none, and none was added. | **2026-10-02 — phase 4's prompt, ticker and evaluation — and where the catalogue ended** | **Prompt:** two instruction lines, 37 in all, after the consent gate's two and before the turn budget. One for the Schedules window — `manage_schedules` works its own controls, saved setups are the user's own (list before acting, change only when asked), `schedule:true` goes where `ids` would, and `make_schedule` sets formats, order, calculated columns, the filter logic and itemise; one for placing — `manage_markups` places at a point that is named, **the point is taken on the box, not on a picked surface, so say so**, place one only when asked, since a placed markup stays until the user deletes it or reverts the reply (phase 4's line said the reply's revert does not remove it; the follow-up reworded it — the revert takes away the markups that reply placed). Two place a markup on top of a named element and are graded on what the Markups card then holds (`markups: {measures, spots}`) and, for the spot, on the height it stands at against the element's own `bbox.max_z` (`spotZ`, to 1.5 mm) — so the dry run proves, in the real viewer, that the named point is where the box says. One asks for the rows of a schedule that is not open: nothing may change, and the reply has to say why. The harness has no Schedules window (its windows are not the app's main window, so the toolbar's own call opens none), so that window's undo, templates, saved setups and its three asking calls have no case; the unit tests and the e2e hold them. `VIEW_FINGERPRINT` carries the Markups card's two lists, so "nothing moved" also means nothing was placed; `gradeView` checks `markups`; `NEGATION` takes the typographic apostrophe. **Not widened:** the write-claim detector does not know a saved schedule setup as one of the app's own things, so *"Renamed the saved setup …"* would be read as a write claim — no case can produce the sentence. **Run, with no API:** oracle **66 / 66**, null **0 / 66**, each into a fresh scratch folder with no report written. **Where the catalogue ended** (UTF-8 bytes of the cached prefix): the tools block 47 127 → **54 448** (36 → 37 tools), the contract 20 263 → **21 468** (181 → 183 lines); 8 526 B more, about 2 130 tokens, written once per cache. **After the follow-up: 54 774 and 21 497** — +326 B in the tools block (the `schedule` target's description on three tools, `manage_schedules`' and `manage_markups`' descriptions) and +29 B in the contract (the placing line). The per-turn view state is unchanged. |
| **2026-10-02 — deliberately left out of the parity work, and why: column widths and the appearance panel, panel sizes, card folding, the chat's own controls — and a picked surface** | The owner's direction is that the assistant should be able to do whatever the user can do in the app. After four phases these remain, each **left out on purpose**, with phase 3's row for what stays the user's alone by decision (the key and Preferences, quit / reload / DevTools, the consent clicks — to which this phase adds the Schedules window's Delete, Print and Open — and anything that writes model data). **In the Schedules window:** column widths — a resize drag, auto-fit, equal width; the appearance panel (title shown, grid lines, zebra, font, bold heading); colour rules; a heading's orientation; thousands separators and the rest of a number's format beyond its decimals and unit; a group footer's style and blank line, and a descending group; `countDistinct` as a total; and the print layout. They are how one schedule looks in one window or on one sheet of paper: a question is never answered by one, the audit put their value low, and `make_schedule` **keeps** every one the user set when it changes the open schedule. A calculated value's **definition** cannot be taken away (`removeColumns` takes its column; the editor's own delete has no counterpart) — the definition is inert without a column. A saved setup whose name is longer than 200 characters or holds a control character can be counted but not named. **In the main window:** dragging the sidebar's three sections and the two panels to a size, and folding a property-card section — layout, session-only, and nothing a result depends on; the chat panel's own controls (reply-to, the suggestions' fold, its size, closing it) — the conversation is the user's side of the exchange, and a turn that closed its own panel would hide its own answer; and placing a markup on a **picked surface** or a snapped corner — a tool has no pointer, so it names a box or a point, and says so. **Also not reachable, by construction rather than decision:** closing the Schedules window (it opens it), the native menu (About, Check for updates, Window › Schedules — the last has a tool route), and reading which spot tags are expanded (it can set either state, idempotently). **The catalogue.** The audit estimated the whole parity work at about +13 kB over the pre-parity catalogue (32 049 B, 31 tools). It ended at **54 448 B, 37 tools: +22 399 B** — phases 1–3 +15 078, this phase +7 321 (`make_schedule` +2 635 for seven inputs and the formula grammar, `manage_schedules` 2 026, `manage_markups` +1 699, the four tools that take `schedule` +960) — and the contract at 21 468 B (37 instruction lines). The estimate was low mostly because what only asks has to say so at length in each description: that is the part a model acts on. |
| **2026-10-02 — the refactor pass after the parity work: nothing visible; the trace's planner in a module of its own, which imports nothing from `./trace`; two pin tests — and phase 4's review leftovers** | The owner: *"After you done, look for refactor opportunities."* A read-only survey at `157bdfb` sorted what it found into three classes; this is its class A — what is invisible and behaviour-preserving — less the shared test harness, which was left for later. Class B (the owner's choice: `CLAUDE.md`'s size, the composer's re-renders, splitting `shell.ts`, `view.ts` and `smoke.spec.ts`) and class C (leave) were not started. **The rule of the pass:** no markup, class name, `data-role`, copy or style string changes; no store action changes what it does; nothing the guard test pins is edited for it; and an item that turned out not to be a pure refactor would have been left undone and said so — none did. **The planner's module boundary.** `ai/trace.ts` was 1 157 lines holding two different things: the port of the handoff's `ask-thinking-hex.jsx` — its curves, durations and sizes, and `Reply`, `Piece` and `hexState` — which is read beside that file, line reference by line reference; and the layout planner — the lattice on whole device pixels, how many elements a cell stands for, the read grid and the filter grid, the rows under an answer and the cells that fly into them — which is the app's own, the handoff having one fixed table of 412 cells in its place. The planner is `ai/trace-plan.ts` now (386 lines; `trace.ts` 857): `REF`, `TraceLayout`, `Grid`, `Lattice`, `traceLattice`, `columnsIn`, `planRead`, `readCellOf`, `planRules`, `rowLayout` and the two caches. **Moved verbatim** — assembled by a script from the committed file, never retyped, and checked by another: all 92 top-level declarations are in exactly one of the two files with their text unchanged; the only edited lines are one doc comment that described two constants and is now two, and `export` on the four plan types and on `readPlanFor` / `rulePlanFor`, which `traceFrame` now imports. **`trace-plan.ts` imports nothing from `./trace`.** `trace.ts` imports the planner, so an import back would be a cycle, and `REF` calls `J` while the planner loads: whichever of the two files was reached second would run against constants that are not initialised yet — and which one that is depends on who imports what first, so it would work until an import order changed. So the five leaf values both files need — `clamp`, `J`, `MATRIX_TOP`, `MONO_CH`, `ROW_CELLS` — are declared in the planner, and `trace.ts` re-exports them with every other public name that moved: `ai/trace` exported 73 names before and exports the same 73, so no importer changed (`App.tsx`, `Trace.tsx`, `ChatPanel.tsx`, `trace-store.ts`, two tests). **Rejected:** a type-only import of `TraceLayout` back from `trace.ts` (erased at build, so no run-time cycle — but "imports nothing" is a rule a reader checks at a glance, and "imports only types" is not); a third module for the five values (three files for one boundary). Held by what already held it, unchanged: `trace.test.ts`, which runs the handoff's own code beside the port at 330 instants, `trace-wiring.test.ts`, `vee-trace.spec.ts` and `frame-triggers.cjs`. **Two pins.** *Every script parses, and none carries a NUL byte* (`tests/unit/scripts-parse.test.ts`). Most of what is under `scripts/` is loaded by no test — the guards, the parity and benchmark harnesses, the audit and the evaluation — and each needs Electron, a build or a real model to run, so a slip in one is found on the day it is needed (`shell-sanity.cjs` could not be parsed for a week). `node --check` compiles a file without running a line of it: one test a file, Node only, about 1.3 s for all 23. The NUL: `scripts/ai-eval.cjs` held two raw 0x00 bytes between the parts of two template-literal map keys — legal JavaScript, and the reason git and every code search treated the file as binary. They are the escape `\u0000` now — the same key at run time, on both sides of the lookup (shown by a run that resumed and skipped exactly the cases an earlier one had recorded) — and the test refuses a raw one. *`design.css` is the design's `<style>` block* (`tests/unit/design-css.test.ts`). The contract says the block is copied as-is, and nothing checked that the copy still was one. It is held line for line, the block found by its tags, line endings normalised and nothing else, **less exactly the three keyframes whose removal is the row of 2026-10-01** — `ifctrace`, `ifcbreathe`, `ifcdot` — and a failure names the first line that differs. **The rest of the pass.** Four exports nothing referenced are gone (`visiblePredicate`, `hostileNotes`, `SchemaCategory`, `AttrKey`; each searched for in `src/`, `tests/` and `scripts/` first; the two in `schedule/ifc/entities.ts` are left, so that ported file stays line-comparable with its source). The visibility frame is computed once: its selector counted hidden elements by building an array of them, and the frame and its reset pill each ran it through a `useMemo` of their own; it counts in a loop now, and `visibilityFrameOnce` — one remembered answer, keyed on the identities of the five inputs — is what both ask (a test holds its values to the old body's on the mock federation, and its passes over the elements to one). `App` no longer renders for the screen-reader summary: that one subscription is `SceneSummary`'s, a component in the same file returning the same `<p id="sgvue-scene" class="sr-only">` in the same place. **Measured** in the built app, on the mock federation, the build before against this one: the element's parent (`main[data-role=stage]`), its index (1, among the stage's six or seven children), the siblings either side of it (the viewport canvas, the overlay), its attributes, its markup and what refers to its id (the canvas's `aria-describedby`, which resolves to it) are identical in four states; and with another element selected, or other storeys isolated, `App` rendered before — the stage's props object was replaced — and does not now, while it still renders when a lane changes (the property card opening, the action bar appearing). Docs that had drifted were brought back: `SYSTEM_SPEC.md`'s deviations table no longer calls itself the complete list (`CLAUDE.md`'s is), installer names read `SGVue-<version>-…`, and the audit's "25 tools" is dated. **Phase 4's review leftovers, closed with it** — nine, each small. The guard is tighter in two places: under `renderer/ai/`, `.up` may be named only as the direct call `.up(` (an alias — `const u = s.up` — a bound form or `.up (` passed all three earlier rules) and — after review — never as a computed member (`s['up']`, `s["up"]`: no dot, so none of the `up` rules saw it), and every `postMessage(` call in `schedule-link.ts` must be one of the literal-typed ones the pinned list is read from. In the Schedules window the operations that act at once (`undo`, `redo`, `apply_template`, `load`, `save`, `rename`, `duplicate`) answer `busy` while a **native** Save or Open dialog of that window is up, as they already did under its own confirm (`dialogUp()`); and `export_schedule` is refused `busy` while that window's confirm or prompt is up (*amended the same day, the last leftovers before the release: or its native Open dialog is — `exportRefusal` asks `fileDialogUp()` as well as `dialogOpen()`, as `manage.ts` does*), so a Save dialog is not raised over a question still waiting — the user's own Export menu cannot be reached under that card (its scrim covers the window and keeps the focus) and its action never asked `exportRefusal`, so a click is as it was. After review, the tool's own sentence for `busy` is true of both causes — *The Schedules window is busy — an export is already under way (its Save dialog may be open), or one of that window's own dialogs is still waiting for an answer* — where it named only the export, and the model passes those words on. `apply_visibility`'s blank-view refusal says what to check by what named the set: the ids, the selection, or what the open schedule lists. The eval's `op-place-measure` is graded on where the measurement was taken, not only on the count (`measureZ`, the spot check's twin; the runner carries each measurement's point in the file's own metres) — measured in the built app through the runner's own expression and the real grader: `top` passes, `centre` and `base` fail with the count right. Two records gained a clause (fourteen gated calls; a stopped turn's placed markup stays, with no reply to revert it). **And two small fixes.** The wall-clock bounds in `marks.test.ts`, which guard a regression three orders of magnitude over, are 1 000 ms — at 100 ms one failed once on a busy machine. And `apply_visibility`'s `selection` input read *"true act ons what the user currently has selected"*: the template put the third person's s on the end of the phrase. **Measured:** the cached prefix is 54 774 B of tools and 21 497 B of contract before and after — the fix moved one letter — and the tools block is rewritten to the cache once. **Tested:** `npm test` 142 files / 2 772 tests (140 / 2 710 before), typecheck and build green, the guarded e2e 62 passed / 6 skipped in two runs, the evaluation's oracle 66 / 66 and null 0 / 66 on scratch flows, `frame-triggers.cjs` all 36. |
| **2026-10-02 — version 1.2.0: the version number and documents only; an installer verified without being run; release notes in `docs/releases/`; and what is published as "system requirements"** | The owner, 2026-10-01: *"Continue until you finish everything, then installer and push both repo and releases. Add system specification on the release landing page as well."* — and, of the reading that "system specification" means the requirements a user needs: *"correct. I will test the installer myself"*. **What 1.2.0 is.** Everything committed since 1.1.0 (`a195f14`): Check for updates opening the product site, the five main-window requests and the bottom row, the ground's height, the update notice, two section cuts, Vee — its name, its mascot, its thinking trace and the stop control — the four phases of the assistant's parity work with the consent gate and the per-reply revert, and the refactor pass. The release itself changes **no behaviour**: `package.json` and the two root entries of `package-lock.json` read 1.2.0, and everything else in it is a document. **The installer.** `npm run dist:win` writes `dist/SGVue-1.2.0-setup.exe`: 115 432 324 bytes, SHA-256 `966b4872ca85e8681e35359f1204242cb0763d967880068ad99e2b8886d8df3d`, the assisted NSIS wizard of 2026-09-28 (per-user, no elevation; install, update or repair), x64, **unsigned** (`Get-AuthenticodeSignature`: `NotSigned`), `publish: null`. **A build never runs it** — installing software on a PC is the owner's own act — so it is verified by three readings instead. Its payload, extracted with electron-builder's own 7-Zip, is **75 of 75 files SHA-256-identical** to `dist/win-unpacked` (409 159 247 bytes), on which `npm run test:packaged` passes and whose version the e2e suite's packaged spec reads as 1.2.0. The `.exe`'s version resource reads FileVersion and ProductVersion `1.2.0`, product `SGVue`, company `Yong Yen`. And `app.asar`'s own header lists `node_modules`, `out` and `package.json` and nothing else, its `package.json` reading `1.2.0`. `out/` is the production build that was packaged: 94 of 94 files identical to the copies in the archive, and no `__sgvueDev` in the bundle. **Release notes are a file from now on**, `docs/releases/<version>.md`, in the published notes' own voice; 1.1.0's were written straight onto its release page and are not in the repository. **What is published as system requirements** — on the product site, in the notes and on the releases page — is `docs/SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §3 said for a user, and only what is true of the published build: Windows 10 or 11, 64-bit; 8 GB for files up to about 100 MB and 16 GB recommended for 200 MB files or several models; any graphics card with WebGL2, an NVIDIA card used automatically; `.ifc` and `.ifczip`, a single file larger than 600 MB not opened; about 110 MB to download and 390 MB installed — megabytes as Windows counts them, which is also how the site's own download line counts; and no internet needed to view models, with the two things that use it. **Read against the code for this, and noted in §3:** the spec's "warning around 600 MB of model data; loading refused above 1.5 GB" was never built — the 600 MB that is enforced is the per-file size limit (`MAX_FILE_BYTES`, `shared/upload.ts`) — so neither figure is published as a memory threshold. **No macOS build** was made for 1.2.0 and none is published, so nothing public mentions one. **Not claimed anywhere public:** that the assistant's answers were tested. No API key is used in development, so its new abilities are held by the unit tests, the e2e suite's scripted turns and the evaluation's no-API oracle, never by a live conversation. **The site's pictures** are the built-in demo building only, taken from the production build through the guarded e2e runner at twice the display's scale; the reply in the Ask Vee picture is a scripted turn — the question and the sentence are written, the tool ran on the demo building through the app's own executor, so its counts, its status and its rows are the app's. |
| **2026-10-05 — SGVue is open source under the Apache License 2.0, published as a fresh public repository, `sgvue/sgvue`, whose first commit is the prepared tree, authored as Yong Yen** | The owner: *"prepare our sg vue repo for open source on github. propose license for me and setup everything properly like a industry standard."* Asked four questions, the owner chose: **the Apache License 2.0** — permissive, so the parser, georeferencing and schedule modules stay reusable by anyone, with an explicit patent grant and inbound-equals-outbound contribution terms (its section 5), and compatible with everything that ships (web-ifc's MPL-2.0 as a Larger Work, the OFL fonts as a separate work); **a fresh start** — a new public repository in the `sgvue` organisation whose first commit is the prepared tree, because every commit of the private repository names a personal account and address, so that history is not published; and **macOS installers built by GitHub** (free macOS runners for a public repository), which the owner tests on a Mac before they are published. **Alternatives rejected: (1) MIT** — the same freedom without the patent grant or the contribution clause; **(2) GPL-3.0** — every fork stays open, but closed reuse of the modules is ruled out; **(3) making the private repository public** — its commit metadata. **Prepared in a worktree, from a read-only audit made the same day (not part of the repository):** the real Windows profile name out of 19 lines in 7 files (`Jane Doe` / `JANEDO~1` keeps each test's meaning — a long spelling with a space and its 8.3 short form); the real models' Revit proxy and storey names replaced by synthetic ones of the same shape (`Family:Type:ElementId`; the same elevations; a long level name of the same length, so `rowLayout`'s numbers hold); the earlier Mac's home paths replaced by the projects' names — Marumi and Aquila, the author's earlier viewers, and ifcgref; `LICENSE` — the canonical text, byte-identical to three copies in `node_modules`; `NOTICE`; `package.json`'s `license`, `homepage`, `repository`, `bugs` and `engines` (`>=22.12`, what vitest 5 and Vite 7 need) — and no `productName`, which would move the app's data folder (2026-09-24) — with npm's own four-line update of the lockfile's root; `electron-builder.yml`'s `copyright`, stated; README rewritten for a stranger (what is sent where, exactly; download; build from source; licence; credits); CONTRIBUTING, CODE_OF_CONDUCT (Contributor Covenant 2.1, reports through the private reporting form), SECURITY (GitHub's private vulnerability reporting), SUPPORT, CHANGELOG and `docs/RELEASING.md`; issue and pull-request templates, and no `CODEOWNERS`, which would have to name a personal account; Dependabot for npm and Actions, weekly, minor and patch grouped, with no version updates for `electron`, `three` or `web-ifc` — each upgrade is measured first; `.editorconfig`, `.nvmrc` (24) and `.gitignore` lines for the agents' working folders. **Measured:** typecheck, `npm test` and `npm run build` pass; `npx electron-builder --win --dir` puts the three licence files in `resources/`, byte-identical, and `app.asar`'s top level is still `node_modules`, `out` and `package.json`, with the same nine packages. |

| **2026-10-05 — the third-party notices are generated from what ships, held to it by `npm test`, and shipped beside `app.asar` in every installer** | `scripts/third-party-notices.cjs` writes `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` from five sources. **(1) `app.asar`'s node_modules:** `package.json`'s `dependencies` and their tree, resolved as Node resolves — the nine packages the archive holds, measured — and code vendored inside them (the SDK's copy of neoqs, BSD-3-Clause). **(2) What Vite bundles:** every package `src/` imports for its value (TypeScript's parser; type-only imports and CSS `@import`s handled), with its tree, less the `dependencies` `src/main` and `src/preload` leave to node_modules — the same eleven packages a source-mapped build's chunks contain (measured once with `--sourcemap`), plus the fonts and the helper code Vite writes (its preload helper; Rollup's CommonJS helpers). **(3) exceljs's browser build:** its source map lists 68 packages; the 27 npm installs here are read from node_modules (the installed version may differ from the one compiled in, and the file says so); the other 41 come from `scripts/third-party-notices.json`, each read from its npm registry tarball and checked against the registry's integrity — `elliptic` 6.5.4 and `core-js` 3.33.0 exactly, the versions the bundle itself records, the rest at the registry's latest on that day, since the map records none — seven of them from their README's licence section, as they ship no licence file. **(4) `web-ifc.wasm`:** the C++ libraries web-ifc 0.0.77's CMake fetches at pinned commits (fast_float, tinynurbs, GLM, earcut.hpp, CDT, spdlog with its bundled fmt, stduuid), read from their repositories. **(5)** Electron's licence, `elevate.exe` (the Windows build's helper), and ifcgref. Each component gets its version, licence, where it ships, its copyright lines and its text — each distinct text once, the components that share it listed above it with their own copyright lines (25 texts for 104 components). The notices that carry a duty come first: web-ifc's MPL-2.0 and where its 0.0.77 source is (tag `0.77` of `ThatOpen/engine_web-ifc`), SQLite's public domain (3.49.1 in sql.js's wasm), the IBM Plex fonts' OFL, and where Chromium's notices are. `standardwebhooks` (MIT) and `saxes` (ISC) ship no licence file: the standard text with the holder from their `author`, said so. The data names the exceljs and web-ifc versions it was read for, and the generator refuses to run against others, so an upgrade cannot silently keep stale texts. `tests/unit/third-party-notices.test.ts` fails when the file is not what the generator writes (shown failing on a one-word edit). `electron-builder.yml` lists `LICENSE`, `NOTICE` and `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` in `extraResources` — `resources/` on Windows, `SGVue.app/Contents/Resources/` on macOS — and excludes them, and the new root documents, from the archive. **Found doing it:** on macOS electron-builder deletes Electron's `LICENSE` and `LICENSES.chromium.html` from the build's output (`app-builder-lib`, `out/electron/electronMac.js:219–220`), where on Windows it keeps both beside `SGVue.exe` (`LICENSE` as `LICENSE.electron.txt`). So a mac-only `extraResources` copies the two from the installed Electron (`node_modules/electron/dist/`, where Electron's macOS distribution keeps them beside `Electron.app`) into `SGVue.app/Contents/Resources/`, under the Windows build's names, and the notices say they are there; the Windows output is unchanged. No macOS build was made here, so that both files are in the bundle is to be confirmed on the first `dist:mac` (`docs/RELEASING.md`, `docs/SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §11). **Alternative rejected:** a hand-written list — it is stale on the next upgrade, and nothing would say so. |
| **2026-10-05 — macOS builds are ad-hoc signed: `mac.identity: '-'`, `hardenedRuntime: false`** | Apple-silicon code must carry at least an ad-hoc signature, and electron-builder rewrites the bundle after Electron's own signature (Info.plist, the executable's name, the resources), so with `identity: null` (Phase 10) a downloaded arm64 build is reported as *"damaged and can't be opened"*, with only `xattr -cr` past it. `'-'` makes electron-builder 26 sign ad hoc — `app-builder-lib`, `out/mac/MacTargetHelper.js:37–43`, `if (qualifier === "-")` — and that code warns that ad-hoc signing with the hardened runtime needs the `disable-library-validation` entitlement or the app fails to launch. The hardened runtime is only what notarisation requires, and notarisation needs a Developer ID anyway, so it is off. A downloader then gets Gatekeeper's "could not verify" prompt once and, on macOS 15 and later, allows the app in System Settings → Privacy & Security → Open Anyway — Control-click → Open no longer exists there (`README.md`, `docs/SYSTEM_SPEC.md` §3). The identity stays explicit, so a certificate in the keychain is never picked up. Nothing else about the macOS build changed. **Not testable here** (no Mac): the owner checks the first build from the release workflow. Owed: an Apple Developer ID and notarisation. |
| **2026-10-05 — `sgvue/releases` is a permanent public channel; CI builds the installers and publishes nothing** | Every installed copy from 1.2.0 on asks `api.github.com/repos/sgvue/releases/releases/latest` for the newest version once at each launch, refusing redirects and counting only a 200 (2026-10-01). So that repository stays public and is never renamed, transferred or deleted — a rename answers with a redirect, which every installed copy would read as failure and fall silent — and every release is published there, as the newest non-pre-release, tagged `vX.Y.Z`. Releases stay hand-made (`docs/RELEASING.md`): the source repository's `.github/workflows/release.yml`, on a `v*` tag or by hand, builds the Windows installer and both DMGs on GitHub's runners with `CSC_IDENTITY_AUTO_DISCOVERY=false`, uploads them as workflow artifacts, writes their SHA-256 into the run's summary and records a build-provenance attestation for each (`actions/attest-build-provenance`, with only `id-token: write` and `attestations: write` beside `contents: read`) — and publishes nothing. The owner tests, the maintainer publishes, betas as pre-releases, which GitHub's latest skips. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs `npm ci`, typecheck, test and build on Windows, macOS and Ubuntu for pushes to `main` and every pull request, with read-only permissions and one run per branch at a time; Ubuntu is advisory, because Linux has never been a target. Every action is pinned to a full commit SHA with its version in a comment. The Electron end-to-end suite is not in CI: it needs a real GPU and runs only under its guard. |
| **2026-10-05 — public commits are authored and committed as `Yong Yen <releases@sgvue.invalid>`, the project's public commit identity** | The owner is credited as Yong Yen (2026-09-24). A commit's author and committer travel with the history, so the public repository's commits carry the project's public commit identity: that name and an address in the reserved `.invalid` domain, which reaches no one and links to no account — set in the clone, never globally (`docs/RELEASING.md`). The account that pushes, and the one that creates a release, are shown publicly all the same — the activity feed, and the release's `author`, which is in the very data the update check fetches; that is accepted where it cannot be done through automation. No file holds a personal account, address or link: no `CODEOWNERS`, and conduct and security reports go through the repository's private reporting form, with no e-mail address. |
| **2026-10-05 — LF in every working copy: `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto eol=lf`** | A fresh checkout on Windows — Git for Windows' `core.autocrlf=true`, which GitHub's Windows runners share — wrote every text file with CRLF, and `tests/readonly-guard.test.ts`, whose patterns over the source are written for LF (`/\n {2}revertTurn: … \},\n/`), failed a test: measured in the new worktree, where the long-lived checkout, its files written LF, passed. With `eol=lf` every platform checks out LF whatever its setting. The index was LF throughout already, so `git add --renormalize .` changes nothing. The binary types the repository holds — PNG, JPEG, WebP, BMP, ICO, ICNS, WOFF2 — are marked binary; `.ifc` stays text (IFC-SPF is ISO 10303-21 text, the two fixtures are generated text and the tests read them as UTF-8). `build/installer.nsh` needs no exception: electron-builder's own NSIS templates are LF and compile on Windows. |
| **2026-10-05 — the public repository, `sgvue/sgvue`, is the source of truth: every push is public, commits carry the project's public commit identity, the privacy search runs before every push, and `sgvue/releases` stays the download channel** | SGVue was published the same day: one commit, `27fb7ee` — this repository's first, the tree the rows above prepared, after its review — authored as Yong Yen. From then on the work happens here: the maintainer's checkout has this repository as `origin`, and the private repository keeps the history before publication as an archive, which is not published. **A push is public at once and cannot be taken back** — rewritten history still survives in clones, forks and caches — so every commit is authored and committed as `Yong Yen <releases@sgvue.invalid>`, set in the clone (the row above), and before every push the **privacy search** runs over what is about to be pushed, for personal accounts, e-mail addresses and paths (a home folder and its short form) and for anything that names a real project: none may appear, and the owner appears only as Yong Yen. Switched on with the repository: private vulnerability reporting, secret scanning with push protection, Dependabot alerts and security updates, CodeQL's default setup, a read-only default token for workflows, and a ruleset, "Protect main", that blocks deleting `main` and force-pushing to it. Releases are unchanged: installers are still published by hand on `sgvue/releases` (the row above), which every installed copy asks for the newest version. |
| **2026-10-05 — Dependabot proposes no major version update for any dependency, in either ecosystem** | Within minutes of publishing, Dependabot opened four major-upgrade pull requests on the public repository — TypeScript 5.9.3 → 7.0.2, Vite 7.3.6 → 8.3.2, `@types/node` 22 → 26 and `@vitejs/plugin-react` 5 → 6 — beside the grouped minor-and-patch one. A major upgrade of the toolchain is a deliberate change, measured before it lands, as Electron's, three.js's and web-ifc's already are — not a bot's pull request (and Vite 8 is the version electron-vite excludes, the stack's row above). So `.github/dependabot.yml` ignores `version-update:semver-major` for `dependency-name: '*'` in both its blocks, npm and GitHub Actions; the four per-package rules stay, with their reasons. **Security updates are unaffected.** GitHub's Dependabot options reference says that `update-types` affects version updates only, not security updates (under `allow`; its `ignore` section takes the same `version-update:` values and does not repeat it), and Dependabot's own source applies only an ignore rule's explicit `versions` to a security update (`dependabot/dependabot-core`, `common/lib/dependabot/config/ignore_condition.rb`: `ignored_versions` returns the rule's `versions` alone when the job is a security update). GitHub also documents that `ignore` can apply to security updates, which is why the rule names the update type and never the dependency alone. |
| **2026-10-08 — with the canvas grid off, the ground veil is not drawn; the opaque ground stays, with its shadow** | Asked for by the owner in these words: *"When off the canvas grid, please dont show the semi-opacity plane filter."* The "semi-opacity plane filter" is 2026-09-24's ground veil (the row "the ground is two layers in the opaque list"): the same plane as the ground, blended over every batch at 0.6, which is what draws an opaque part below grade at 40 % of its contrast. **The reading taken, which the owner may still correct:** only the veil goes; the opaque layer under it — the ground's colour, and the building's shadow on it — stays. Dropping the whole ground was the alternative, and would have taken the shadow with it. **How** (`viewer/scene.ts`): `buildScene` builds the veil with `visible = groundGrid`, and the rig's `setGroundGrid` sets it beside the grid helper's. `setTheme` rebuilds only the helper, so the veil keeps its state through a theme change, and `viewer-core`'s `buildRig` hands every rebuilt rig `groundGridOn` (the 2026-09-24 row on where the flag lives), so a rescale keeps it too. **Nothing else is needed for a redraw:** `viewer-core`'s `setGroundGrid` already calls `invalidate()`, and the shadow map does not change — three 0.186's shadow pass draws casters only (`ShadowBaseNode`, `object.castShadow === true`, the VSM exception aside), and neither the veil nor the helper casts; the opaque ground has `receiveShadow` of its own, so it takes the shadow without the veil, which only ever re-blended the same shadowed colour over it. The assistant's `toggle_display` `groundGrid` calls the button's own `setGroundGrid`, so it follows with no code of its own (measured below). The dev draw count (`debug().calls`) is one less with the grid off. **What else the veil did:** it wrote the depth the design's opaque ground wrote, and every transparent pass after it met that depth. So with the grid off, what is below grade is drawn as it is — an opaque part at full contrast, and its ordinary edges, see-through parts, glass and annotations below grade too (measured below: a storey's level ring under grade) — and from above the ground is a backdrop that hides nothing. From below nothing changes (both layers are `FrontSide`). **Measured** (scratchpad harness under `safe-run.cjs`: the mock, 1280 × 820, ratio 1, the 3D view, the canvas alone — `[data-role="stage"] > *:not([data-role="viewport"])` hidden — 980 × 820 = 803 600 px, the build before against this one, each theme its own run, every frame at 60 fps): **grid on, all eight states byte-identical** (the whole federation; again after off and on; ARC + STR; the same by the assistant's tool; levels on; the bare ground; ARC + STR with levels on; and after a rescale, below). **Grid off:** with the whole federation, 1 586 px change (dark max 33 levels, light 84), in two strips along the site's outer edge; the app's own picker, cast through each, hits Turf Area S, E or N (boxes −0.1 … 0 m, astride the ground plane at −0.02 m) for 769 of them — 486 below the plane, the other 283 within 3 px of such a hit — and nothing for the remaining 817, each within 1–4 px of a below-plane hit: the turf's below-grade sides, edges and silhouette. With only ARC and STR shown, 6 954 / 6 958 px (dark / light, max 78 / 128; 6 959 in either): the eight footings (−1.0 … −0.5 m) 4 241, the ground slab's rim (−0.2 … 0 m) 1 623, eight L1 column stubs below grade 85, and 1 010 rays that hit nothing, each within 1 px of a below-plane hit — but one, on a far IFC gridline. Luma rises in dark (mean +7.5 / +24.4) and falls in light (−33.9 / −57.6): the parts no longer drawn toward the ground's colour. With levels on, 1 586 px again: the `Foundation` ring at −1 m lies under the turf; with levels on and only ARC and STR shown, 2 112 ring pixels are drawn that were not — that ring, which the veil's depth hid. **The rebuild:** with the grid off, unloading the site model rescaled the scene (1.001 → 0.656) and replaced the rig (a new ground mesh); the new rig's veil came up hidden and followed the toggle both ways, and with the grid on the frame is the build before's. **The shadow:** in the ARC + STR view the building's shadow covers 28 146 / 28 156 px of the ground plane, darker by 12 / 40 levels on average, and every one of those pixels is in the build before's shadow too, with the same value; the build before counts 261 / 262 more, every one a pixel whose ray hits no element: plain shadowed ground there, and here the outline edges of the footings and the slab's rim, which the veil's depth hid. **One more difference, not below grade:** on the bare ground (every element hidden), 258 px — the far IFC gridlines, drawn 1 cm above grade and 3 cm above the ground plane, used to lose some samples to the veil's depth, and with the grid off they draw whole; with the grid on, 0. **Tested:** three cases in `tests/unit/ground-level.test.ts` — built with the grid off the veil is hidden and the ground drawn; `setGroundGrid` both ways; `setTheme` keeps it — each of which fails against the build before's `scene.ts`; the guarded e2e suite passes whole (62 passed, 6 skipped), the two canvas-grid cases in `smoke.spec.ts` unchanged among them. |
| **2026-10-08 — the federation is assembled in map space: each model's own world → map operation, P = M_boot ∘ Site_boot, and each model streamed through M_i⁻¹ ∘ P** | Reported by the owner in these words: *"Are you able to identify why the shared coordinates are not align with this image?"* → *"My ifc are based on site placement while other are based on ifcmapconversion."* → *"Propose the fixes with the simplest yet workable stable solution to turn this app to international ifc standard for shared coordinates alignment and units."* Five rules were proposed and approved (*"Yes, all five"*); this is part 1 — rules 1, 2, 3 and 5. **The defect:** since 2026-09-20 every model was streamed through the boot model's site frame, so the federation lined up by **world** coordinates. A Revit "Shared Coordinates" export writes its map position on `IfcSite.ObjectPlacement`; a "Project Base Point" or "Survey Point" export with an EPSG code writes it in `IfcMapConversion` over a site near the file's zero ([revit-ifc Exporter.cs](https://github.com/Autodesk/revit-ifc/blob/IFC_v27.3.0.12/Source/Revit.IFC.Export/Exporter/Exporter.cs#L2940-L3047)). Both are compliant in CORENET X, and by world coordinates they land kilometres apart — measured in the built app on the synthetic pair below, **25 524 m**. The few tools that do align such pairs carry each model into map space through its own operation, identity when it has none — IfcOpenShell's viewer ([Federation.h](https://github.com/IfcOpenShell/IfcOpenShell/blob/v0.9.0/src/ifcviewer/Federation.h)). **The rule** (`shared/georef.ts`, pure): `mapPlacement(georef)` is the **one** function that says where a model is on the map — `map = T(E·u, N·u, H·u) · Rz(θ) · world`, θ = atan2(XAxisOrdinate, XAxisAbscissa) anticlockwise — the axis need not be a unit vector; identity for no conversion or one that moves nothing (`isIdentityMapConversion`, which reads Revit's zero translation and `(1, 6.12e-17)` axis as the identity, so nothing turns twice). The conversion read is the one whose `SourceCRS` is the 3D `Model` context — or a sub-context of it, judged by its `ParentContext` — else the first (`worker/index-builder.ts`); IFC2X3's `ePset_MapConversion` feeds the same fields. `federationFrame(boot)` is **P = M_boot ∘ Site_boot**, project → map, a federation constant like the offset; `modelFrame(boot, g)` is **M_i⁻¹ ∘ P**, project → model i's world, which keeps `ProjectFrame`'s shape, so `worldToProjectMatrix`, the streamer and `metaOf` (grids, storeys) are unchanged and the streamer is still the only place the frames meet. **Exactness:** when model i's operation equals the boot model's, number for number — the identity included — M⁻¹ ∘ M is not computed and the frame is the boot model's site frame itself, and `composeFrames` hands a `null` side's partner back unchanged; so every model of an identity-operation federation (Revit "Shared Coordinates", the reference model, the mock) and every model placed the way the boot model is streams exactly as before, and P and `frameKey` for an identity-operation boot are exactly what they were. A boot model placed by a map conversion keeps its own stream but gets a new `frameKey` — P, not its local site frame — so a session, link or viewpoint saved against it restores everything but the camera, as designed (2026-09-20). The offset, the Float64 composition and `COORDINATE_TO_ORIGIN = false` are unchanged. A boot model that fails to prepare after it fixed the frame gives the choice back — and the offset its stream may have set — when nothing is loaded and no other model has been streamed in that frame; one that has stands in it, and keeps it. **The base point is P:** `coordsFromGeoref` is P read as a base point (same u, so the readouts and the geometry cannot disagree), and the controller sets it **once, at boot, from the boot model** (`addBatch`), never from a model that joins later — before, the first model to state any georeferencing filled it, whose numbers described its own frame, not the scene's. The Coordinate-system card's chip and caption, the status bar's chip and `basePointSource` read `cardGeoref`: the model whose own base point the four fields are, else the first that states any (the chips' old rule). *(Part 2: they read the store's `bootGeoref` — the declaration that defined P, kept beside it — which stays right after the boot model is unloaded; `cardGeoref` is gone.)* `get_model_info` reports per model `placedBy` (`IfcMapConversion`, `ePset_MapConversion`, `site placement`, `none`), `mapUnit {name, metresPerUnit, assumedMetre}` and `scaleApplied: false`. **No visible change**: no element, control or copy; the card's values change only where they were wrong (a later model's numbers, a map unit other than the metre, a decoy conversion). **Measured:** one synthetic building at the repository's coordinates written eight ways (`scripts/make-tiny-ifc.py` → `tests/fixtures/georef/`: site placement, conversion with `Scale` 0.001 / absent / 1000, in feet, IFC2X3 psets, a survey point with a decoy conversion, and the same with the survey point's conversion on the `Body` sub-context) — every ordered pair of the 56 federates within **1 mm**, vertices, element boxes, grid segments and storey heights, through the real index builder, streamer and `metaOf`; and IfcOpenShell's `util.geolocation` puts every product of (a), (b) and (f) where `mapPlacement` does. Against the build before, streamed under Node: every model alone, every pair of identity-operation models and every pair sharing the boot's operation byte-identical (21 of 81 streams; the other 60 are the mixed pairs this fixes, and the base points of (e) and (g)); in the built app, canvas-only, the mock in 9 states and both fixtures in 6, both themes — **every bitmap identical** once the camera has settled (at 1.6 s one ortho → perspective flight on `tiny.ifc` was 2 px short, and identical at 5 s); and the pairs (a) + (b), (b) + (a), (a) + (f), (g) + (e) loaded into one window: `Slab L1` 25 524 m / 23 453 m / 25 524 m / 13.8 m apart before, **0.0000 m** after. |
| **2026-10-08 — `IfcMapConversion.Scale` is never applied for placement; it is reported as written** | IFC4.3 reads `Scale` as the project-unit ÷ map-unit ratio, 0.001 for a millimetre model in a metre CRS ([IFC4.3 IfcMapConversion](https://standards.buildingsmart.org/IFC/RELEASE/IFC4_3/HTML/lexical/IfcMapConversion.htm)) — but the files disagree. Revit's exporter wrote it absent up to its 25.x releases, 0.001 from 26.1 ([PR #785](https://github.com/Autodesk/revit-ifc/pull/785)) and 1000 from 27.3.0.12 in August 2026, following buildingSMART's validation rule GRF005, which expected 1000 from June 2025 until it was corrected on 31 August 2026 ([ifc-gherkin-rules #522](https://github.com/buildingSMART/ifc-gherkin-rules/issues/522), [PR #538](https://github.com/buildingSMART/ifc-gherkin-rules/pull/538)); the specification-model examples write 1000 and the published Annex E examples none. Applied literally, one cohort of files is drawn 1 000 or 10⁶ times too large. web-ifc has already applied the project length unit inside `flatTransformation`, so nothing is left for `Scale` to do: E, N and H are multiplied by the map unit (next row) and `Scale` is recorded on the index and in `get_model_info` exactly as written, with `scaleApplied: false`. The report's decoder — reading `Scale` as evidence against the expected ratio — is not built; it belongs with part 2's guards. Fixtures (b), (c) and (d) differ only in `Scale` (0.001, absent, 1000) and stream byte-identically. |
| **2026-10-08 — the map unit u comes from `IfcProjectedCRS.MapUnit`; when the file names none, it is the metre** | E, N and H are in the map CRS's unit, which `IfcProjectedCRS.MapUnit` names: an `IfcSIUnit` by its prefix, an `IfcConversionBasedUnit` through its `ConversionFactor` — the international foot 0.3048 m, the US survey foot 1200/3937 m, which differ by 2 ppm, 25 mm at a 12 km easting — read by the same `unitFactor` as the project's unit assignment. IFC2X3's `ePset_ProjectedCRS` can name it only as text (the OSArch template's `METRE`), so `lengthUnitFromLabel` (`shared/units.ts`) reads the metre with any SI prefix and both feet; a name it does not know is read as metres, and `get_model_info` says `assumedMetre`. **Absent, it is the metre**, not IFC4.3's project length unit ([IFC4.3 IfcProjectedCRS](https://standards.buildingsmart.org/IFC/RELEASE/IFC4_3/HTML/lexical/IfcProjectedCRS.htm)): Revit leaves `MapUnit` empty unless it knows a map-unit string while still writing eastings in metres ([Exporter.cs](https://github.com/Autodesk/revit-ifc/blob/IFC_v27.3.0.12/Source/Revit.IFC.Export/Exporter/Exporter.cs#L4045-L4122)), and the published Annex E examples pair a millimetre project with metre eastings and no unit ([Annex E](https://standards.buildingsmart.org/IFC/RELEASE/IFC4_3/HTML/annex_e/placements-and-gis-referencing/geographic-referencing-utm.html)) — the project-unit reading would take those metre eastings for millimetres and put every such file a thousandth of the way from the map's origin to where it is. Fixture (e) states its position in feet under a foot `MapUnit` and lands within 1 mm of the others; the build before read its feet as metres (the base point read `E 40503.468`). |
| **2026-10-08 — `TrueNorth` is never stacked on a map conversion, and in part 1 it places nothing at all** | IFC4 and IFC4.3 say that when a coordinate operation exists the context's `TrueNorth` is informational and loses to it ([IFC4.3 IfcGeometricRepresentationContext](https://standards.buildingsmart.org/IFC/RELEASE/IFC4_3/HTML/lexical/IfcGeometricRepresentationContext.htm)), and Revit derives `TrueNorth` and the conversion's X axis from one angle ([Exporter.cs](https://github.com/Autodesk/revit-ifc/blob/IFC_v27.3.0.12/Source/Revit.IFC.Export/Exporter/Exporter.cs#L2985-L2993)) — so applying both would turn every "Project Base Point" export with an EPSG code twice. `mapPlacement` never reads `TrueNorth`; fixtures (b) to (e) carry one equal to their conversion's angle and federate within 1 mm. The base point keeps 2026-09-20's readout rule — its angle falls back to `TrueNorth` only when nothing places the model with a rotation — which is a readout, and which differs from P only for a boot model whose sole rotation statement is `TrueNorth`: the `WorldCoordinateSystem` case (Revit's IFC2X3 and no-EPSG exports), which is part 2's rule 4. *(Part 2: beside a `WorldCoordinateSystem` that is the map position, `TrueNorth` is the turn when nothing else states one — the rule-4 row below. It still never stacks on a conversion, and without a `WorldCoordinateSystem` it still places nothing.)* |
| **2026-10-08 — CRS names are deliberately not compared** | Two models are aligned by their operations whatever their `IfcProjectedCRS` is called, and never refused or reprojected for a different name. Every regime surveyed fixes one CRS per project at the start (CORENET X: SVY21 and the Singapore Height Datum), `IfcMapConversion` performs no projection, and the names are unreliable: the same Singapore grid is `EPSG:3414` or the compound `EPSG:6927` ([epsg.io/6927](https://epsg.io/6927)), exporters write placeholders (`Local CRS`, `Unknown SRS`) or free text, and some systems exist only as WKT — a string comparison would split compliant federations and could not compare WKT at all. The CRS chips still name what the file declares (2026-09-18). What to say about a model that cannot be lined up is part 2's. *(Part 2: the Coordinate-system card's one-line note, below.)* |
| **2026-10-08 — rule 4: with no map conversion, the `Model` context's `WorldCoordinateSystem` is the map position, `TrueNorth` turns it only when nothing else does, and beside a conversion it is undone first** | Coordinates part 2 — rule 4 of the five the owner approved (*"Yes, all five"*). **Why:** Revit writes the chosen point's Easting, Northing and Elevation as the `Location` of the 3D `Model` context's `WorldCoordinateSystem`, in project length units and with no axis turn, whenever it writes no `IfcMapConversion` — its Survey Point, Project Base Point and Internal Origin exports with no EPSG code, and every IFC2X3 export — and for the Project Base Point and the Internal Origin the turn to true north is only in the context's `TrueNorth` ([Exporter.cs](https://github.com/Autodesk/revit-ifc/blob/IFC_v27.3.0.12/Source/Revit.IFC.Export/Exporter/Exporter.cs#L2940-L3047)). A reader that ignores it puts such a file at its local origin: in the built app, the synthetic Project-Base-Point file beside the site-placed one stood 26 km away, and its card read `E 4 · N -2.5 · Z 0` — the local site offset — under `IfcSite placement`. **The engine question, settled by measurement:** web-ifc 0.0.77 applies the `WorldCoordinateSystem` to no placement — neither its move nor its turn. One slab, its site at the origin, streamed with the identity, with the WCS moved 1 km / 2 km / 300 m, turned 30°, and both: the very same `flatTransformation` four times, and `GetCoordinationMatrix` the identity (`COORDINATE_TO_ORIGIN` is false); our `placementMatrix` composes only the `PlacementRelTo` chain too, so grids and storeys agree with the geometry. A test pins it with an inline STEP file, so an upgrade that starts applying it fails before the map position is added twice (`docs/TRAPS.md`). **The rule** (`mapPlacement`, `shared/georef.ts`): with no conversion and a WCS that is not the identity, M = the WCS — origin through the length unit, its own turn if it has one — then, only when nothing else states a turn (no site turn, no WCS turn, no tilt of either), the turn `TrueNorth` implies, θ = atan2(x, y) of its direction (`trueNorthDeg`). **The sign:** `TrueNorth` is north in the project's own axes, and Rz(θ) must take it onto the map's +Y: Rz(θ)·(sin θ, cos θ) = (0, 1) — a unit test checks exactly that, and that the result equals the operation the same export with an EPSG code states in its conversion (Revit writes the X axis and `TrueNorth` from one angle). IfcOpenShell's `get_true_north` reads the synthetic `TrueNorth` as +43.4103°, "how far project north turns anticlockwise to reach true north", which is θ's negative. Without a WCS, `TrueNorth` still places nothing: two local files, one carrying a `TrueNorth` for its solar studies, would otherwise be turned apart. A WCS that only tilts, at the origin, is the identity, as a tilted site is (`isIdentityWcs` mirrors `isIdentitySitePlacement`, after the review): it places nothing and gives no base point; a tilt still keeps `TrueNorth` out where the WCS does move. **Beside a conversion** — current Revit never writes the pair (its WCS is zero whenever it writes a conversion), and IFC calls it ambiguous — IfcOpenShell's reading: the WCS undone before the conversion, M = C ∘ WCS⁻¹ (its `auto_xyz2enh` applies `inv(wcs)`, then the Helmert transform; web-ifc not applying the WCS, nothing else needs undoing), and `get_model_info` says `ambiguous`, in a sentence. Read either other way the building would stand |w| or 2|w| away. Rule 5 holds: `TrueNorth` never stacks on a conversion. **Labels:** `placedBy` gains `WorldCoordinateSystem`; `detectMethod` names `WorldCoordinateSystem`, or `WorldCoordinateSystem + IfcSite placement` when the site placement moves the project too, after the conversion's pattern (the caption's two new strings — the 2026-09-20 deviation's amendment); `sources` gains `WorldCoordinateSystem`, and `source` takes it before `IfcSite`. The base point reads E, N, Z and the angle off P. **Exactness:** an identity WCS leaves every reading as it was, field for field (a unit test compares the whole old shapes), and the eight part-1 fixtures regenerate byte-identically and read `wcs: null`. **Measured:** three new fixtures from the same generator, the synthetic set only — (i) Revit Project Base Point with no EPSG code (a local site offset of (4, −2.5) m with no turn, the WCS at the base point, `TrueNorth` the turn), (j) the same in IFC2X3, (k) a WCS of (5, 2, 1) m beside a conversion written for IfcOpenShell's reading — and all eleven federate within 1 mm in **all 110 ordered pairs** (vertices, element boxes, grid segments, storey heights) through the real index builder, streamer and `metaOf`. IfcOpenShell agrees product by product: `auto_xyz2enh` for (k), and its own `get_wcs` and `get_true_north`, composed as Revit writes them, for (i) and (j) — `auto_xyz2enh` returns a file with no conversion unmoved. In the built app, (i) + (a) in one window: before, 26 km apart; after, one building, and the card reads (i)'s base point as the synthetic position under `WorldCoordinateSystem + IfcSite placement`. **Two limits, by design.** A Project Base Point or Internal Origin export whose chosen point is at the shared origin — Revit's default — writes an identity `WorldCoordinateSystem`, so its `TrueNorth` stays a readout and is not applied. After a confirmed Replace of the boot model, `bootGeoref` and the caption keep the first version's declaration: the frame is chosen once and a replacement does not reframe. |
| **2026-10-08 — the Coordinate-system card says, in one line, which loaded model could not be lined up** | Offered to the owner as *"A short note in the Coordinate system card. Recorded as a change to the design."*, chosen in these words: *"One-line note on screen"*. **The rule** (`notLinedUp`, `selectors/status.ts`, pure): nothing with one model loaded; else a model is named when its file states **no map position** (`mapPlacement`'s `none`) while another loaded model's does — its world coordinates were taken as map coordinates, wrong for a genuinely local file — or, failing that, when the geometry stream flagged it **far**: its bounding box's centre more than `FAR_PLACEMENT_METRES` (5 km) from the federation offset. `GeometryWarning` gained that distance in `metres`; the renderer writes it onto the index before freezing it (`ModelIndexMeta.farPlacementMetres`), **only for a model that did not set the offset** — the one that did measured against its own first part, which says nothing about the others. **When the boot model is the one with no map position**, the scene's origin is that model's own and every model with a map position lands kilometres from it: the boot model is named and no distance is — a model standing where its own map position puts it is not the one that could not be lined up. The frame's declaration is the store's `bootGeoref` (the row below), so this holds after the boot model is unloaded. **The words:** `Tower B.ifc could not be lined up — it has no map position.` · `STR.ifc could not be lined up — it sits 12.3 km from the others.` · `2 models could not be lined up: Tower B.ifc (no map position), STR.ifc (12.3 km away).` A model is named by its sidebar row's file line (`modelFile`, taken out of `modelRows`: the picked file's name, else the library's, else the index's — a file opened from disk is indexed under its key), through `labelText`, which moved to `shared/fmt.ts` so a selector can use it (`ai/executors/context.ts` re-exports it). **The element:** directly under the caption row, only while there is something to say, a `<span data-role="coords-note">` with the caption span's own style string plus `white-space:nowrap;overflow:hidden;text-overflow:ellipsis`, the whole text as its native `title` (2026-10-01's rule for clipped text); in the card's column it is a block 270 px wide, one 16.8 px line, 12 px under the row. With nothing to say nothing is drawn, and every style string of the card is the design's. `get_model_info` (per model, `georeferencing.notLinedUp`: the reason and the kilometres) and `get_view_state` (`notLinedUp: [{model, reason, km}]`) report the same fact; no new tool. **Measured** (production builds, the real main process, files through the Open dialog's dev gate, 1280 × 820, both themes; this build against the commit before): `a-site-placement.ifc` + `tiny.ifc` — the note reads `tiny.ifc could not be lined up — it has no map position.`, clipped (`scrollWidth` 288 against 270), its `title` whole; the card 294.2 → 323.0 px tall, its crop differing from the build before by the note alone (14 093 px), the same in three captures of this build; with the card closed the window is byte-identical to the build before's. `a-site-placement.ifc` + `b-map-conversion.ifc` — no note, the card byte-identical to the build before's. |
| **2026-10-08 — the Coordinate-system card is read-only; the base point is the boot file's, kept with the frame** | Asked for by the owner in these words: *"Maybe just make the coordinates system toggle a read only, dont let user change anything."* **The card:** the four fields keep their element, class and style string and are `readOnly` — they still take focus and a selection, so a value can be copied (the e2e selects one), and nothing typed reaches them; there is no `onChange`. **Nothing in the app changes the base point:** the store's `setCoord` (the design's `:1039`) is gone, and so are `request_user_action`'s `set_base_point` — zod, the JSON schema's `E` / `N` / `Z` / `angle`, the `PendingAction` member, `performGated`'s branch, the gate (**thirteen** gated calls, the guard's list updated line for line), the ticker's phrase, the prompt's two mentions, the eval case `op-base-point` (65 cases) and the grader's `basePoint` check (the fingerprint still reads it, as "nothing moved") — and the revert part `basePoint`: `coords` is in `NOT_REVERTED`, with its reason. `get_view_state.basePoint.source` is `file` or `none` (`basePointSource(bootGeoref)`). The dev-only hook that set the viewer's base point alone (`__sgvueDev.setCoords`) went too, and so did the helpers that guarded against typed fields (`sameBasePoint`, `hasManualCoords`, `cardGeoref`, `crsChip`'s `manualCoords`). **Where the base point comes from — the part-1 review's deferred item:** the store keeps `bootGeoref`, the boot model's georeferencing — the declaration that defined P — beside `frame`, set with the offset at boot (`setOffset(offset, frame, bootGeoref)`, the one place `coords` is written; it pushes it to the viewer) and cleared with them when nothing is loaded. The base point, both CRS chips, the caption and `basePointSource` are read off it, so after the boot model is unloaded while others stay they stay right (they used to fall back to another model: the caption vanished and the source read `user`), and after a boot model whose geometry failed once another model stood in its frame they describe that frame, the one in use. Cleared with the frame, the base point no longer survives an emptied federation into the next boot (it used to: the next boot found it set and kept it). **The status bar's chip** names the CRS the boot file declares, or the em dash: it no longer shows the design's `SVY21` for a typed base point, and no longer borrows a later model's CRS. **Sessions, share links, viewpoints:** a stored `coords` is **ignored on restore** (`sessionPatch` no longer returns it, `RESTORE_ORDER` has no `coords` step) — the base point always comes from the boot file. **It is still written**, for older builds: a build from before restores a payload's `coords` over its own reading of the same file, which gives it the same numbers for every file it can read and the right base point for one it cannot (a file placed by its `WorldCoordinateSystem`, whose project frame both builds share). Not writing it would have had them keep their own reading — as safe, but wrong for those files. Viewpoints never carried it. **Pinned:** the guard test finds every `set(` / `setState(` under `src/renderer/` whose object names `coords` — exactly one, `setOffset`'s — the federation controller as `setOffset`'s one caller, no tool naming either, no `setCoord` in the store, and `readOnly` with no `onChange` in the card. **Measured** (production builds, both themes, this build against the commit before): the demo's card byte-identical (the design's mock states no base point); the demo's whole window, card closed or open, byte-identical to one of this build's own two renderings — the overlay labels' antialiasing alternates between two states run to run (1 802 / 1 729 px), in this build and the one before alike; the synthetic pair's card byte-identical. The phase-6 parity chain's `spot-C1` step typed the design's base point into the card (`scripts/lib/parity-states.cjs`); it no longer does — the card would refuse it — so from `spot-C1` on the app's states differ from the phase-6 captures: on the mock the spot reads its level in the file's own metres, as the demo always has (`tests/parity/phase6/README.md`). No re-baseline. |
